#528. Why US investors are snapping up Germany's famed Mittelstand

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Increasing amounts of US capital are flowing into Germany's traditional industrial firms and family-owned businesses. As many business owners struggle to find successors, more companies are becoming acquisition targets.

What do the long-established German companies Birkenstock, Viessmann, Linde and ebm-papst have in common? All have fallen into American hands.

Traditional German family-owned businesses and small-to-medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) have become increasingly attractive to US companies and investors.

Last year, US investors were involved in 76 acquisitions and investments in German industrial and manufacturing companies, according to an analysis by financial data provider PitchBook. The transactions were valued at about $29.5 billion (€26.3 billion) in 2025, nearly double the 2022 total. As of August 19, 2026, the figure stood at $23.9 billion.

"From what we are seeing in the market, the interest from US investors in German companies is definitely still there," said Samar-Ahmad Albrecht-Mahmood of Veracity Capital Investments, which advises companies looking to invest in Germany.

German companies, particularly those in the industrial and manufacturing sector, remain attractive, "because many of them are highly specialized, have strong customer relationships and very good technical know-how," he told DW. "In many cases, these are businesses that have built up a strong position in their niche over decades."

At the same time, many German business owners are reaching an age where they are thinking about selling their business, Albrecht-Mahmood underscored, pointing out that "there is often no successor within the family."

"This brings companies onto the market that might not have been available for sale a few years ago," he said.

Notable takeovers this year include ebm-papst, the Mulfingen-based manufacturer of electric motors and fans, which was bought by US company Madison Air for $5.4 billion in August.

In June, the long-established Augsburg-based company Everllence, formerly MAN Energy Solutions, was sold to Bain Capital, with the US investment firm acquiring a 51% stake in the company.

According to Die Deutsche Wirtschaft (DDW), a German business information network focused on the Mittelstand (medium-sized and family-owned companies), no country owns more German firms than the United States. Of the total 7,861 companies identified in the database "German Companies Under Foreign Ownership," 1,750 are currently held by US investors.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

PricewaterhouseCoopers (PwC), one of the world's leading auditing and consultancy organizations, also underscored the growing US investor interest in acquiring German companies. In a report, titled Destination Germany, PwC said that while the number of acquisitions and equity investments has not increased, the financial volume of the deals has jumped.

The report highlighted, in particular, the acquisition of BASF Coatings by the US investment firm Carlyle, in partnership with the Qatar Investment Authority (QIA), for around €7.7 billion.

The deal was struck on October 26, 2025, and closed on June 30 of this year.

According to a recent survey by the American Chamber of Commerce in Germany, about 62% of the US companies active in the country are expecting business conditions to improve over the next three to four years.

Meanwhile, some 52% of them are planning to expand their operations in Germany, up from 49% in 2024.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

At first glance, however, the increase in US acquisitions and equity investments does not seem to align with the decline in US investment flows to Germany last year.

According to the Bundesbank, significantly less investment capital flowed from the US to Germany in 2025 than in the previous year.

While foreign direct investment in Germany has risen overall — from €57.4 billion in 2024 to a total of €86.1 billion last year — investment from the US amounted to only €11.8 billion, nearly 44% less than in 2024.

In contrast, investment from the United Kingdom rose to around €26 billion.

"I would say the trend is continuing, although perhaps not simply in terms of higher deal numbers every year," Albrecht-Mahmood said. "The general interest in good German industrial companies, especially strong Mittelstand businesses, remains high."

Relatively low valuations also play a role, the expert argued. "Compared to the US, we still see situations where German companies are valued more moderately, particularly in the SME segment. This can make acquisitions quite attractive for international buyers."

This article was originally written in German.

#468. US sanctions target Iran's rail sector as Tehran shifts cargo from sea routes to overland corridors

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

As US sanctions expand to Iran's rail sector, Tehran is relying more heavily on overland links with China and Central Asia. But capacity constraints, delays and costs limit rail's ability to replace maritime trade.

The United States has opened another front in its economic pressure campaign against Iran, this time targeting the railways Tehran increasingly relies on as maritime trade becomes more difficult.

On October 1, the US Treasury sanctioned three Iranian railway companies alongside seven automakers and five foreign suppliers of automotive parts as part of its Operation Economic Outcast.

Washington says Iran's rail and automotive industries provide revenue and logistical capacity to the state and are connected to networks involving the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC).

The timing of the move is significant. Since the US maritime blockade sharply restricted Iran's southern trade routes, Tehran has tried to move more goods through its land borders and railway connections with China, Central Asia, Russia and Turkey.

Rail transport has become more important just as Washington decided to target it.

Shahriar Naghizadeh, director general for foreign trade at Iran's railway authority, was quoted by the semi-official Mehr news agency as saying that the country has around 15,000 kilometers (9,300 miles) of main and secondary railway lines, more than 35,000 wagons and nearly 500 diesel locomotives.

The network carried around 44 million tons of freight in the Iranian year ending in March 2026, up from about 40 million tons the previous year, according to Naghizadeh. And authorities are targeting 54 million tons this year.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Iran is linked to neighboring markets through 13 rail crossings, providing overland access to Turkey, Azerbaijan, Afghanistan, Pakistan and onward routes to Central Asia and China via Turkmenistan.

The new US sanctions raise questions about how foreign railway operators, freight forwarders, banks and insurers will continue dealing with Iranian rail companies.

Bloomberg reported in May that freight trains traveling from the Chinese city of Xi'an to Tehran had increased from around one per week before the US blockade to roughly one every three or four days.

In September, a train left China carrying automotive parts, consumer goods and other cargo. It ran through Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan and Turkmenistan before entering Iran at Sarakhs with an expected transit time of 14 to 16 days.

For Iran, such services offer a route to Chinese suppliers that avoids the area around the Strait of Hormuz. But advertised transit times do not necessarily reflect what an importer experiences.

A Chinese freight company told DW that actual delivery times can stretch far beyond advertised schedules because of border inspections, customs procedures and train availability.

It estimated that rail transit along the main route from western China to Tehran would take 50-60 days under current conditions. Road transport along a similar route is around 22 to 25 days.

The gap between a scheduled 14-day transit time and commercial estimates of up to 60 days highlights one of the central challenges facing Iran.

Customs inspections, gauge changes, payment problems and sanctions can all be another potential point of delay.

Increasing the number of trains also requires more locomotives, wagons and cooperation from several transit countries.

Alireza Salavati, managing director of London-based Middle East Analytica, said rail is increasingly useful for Iran but should not be mistaken as a replacement for maritime trade.

"A workable overland route now links China and Iran, but I would not call it a replacement for sea freight," he told DW.

Under normal conditions, ships can move far larger quantities at lower unit costs. Rail becomes more attractive for goods where speed and continuity matter more than the cheapest transport.

That includes automotive components, machinery, electronics and industrial inputs, whose absence can stop an entire production line.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Salavati argues that the effect on companies cannot be measured only in freight charges.

For a manufacturer importing $1 million (€890,000) worth of inputs every 30 days, a 30-day journey means roughly a million dollars of inventory may be tied up in transit.

Cutting the journey to 15 days could reduce that amount significantly, depending on payment arrangements. The reverse can be much more damaging.

If input costs rise while delivery times stretch from 30 to 45 days, companies need more working capital simply to maintain the same production flow, the expert explained, pointing out that a factory can have customers and orders but still have to reduce production because too much cash is trapped in goods that have not arrived.

"Reliability matters as much as speed," Salavati said. "A 15-day train is little use if the container waits a week to depart, or if the bank or insurer will not touch it."

The new US sanctions add another source of uncertainty to that calculation.

Optimizing its railway use would give Iran more options than having to rely on southern ports alone, but the overall scale remains limited.

Washington's sanction strategy is designed to isolate Iran by closing maritime, financial, aviation and now railway channels.

Still, Salavati argues that the same pressure may accelerate Iran's integration with alternative networks.

"Washington's efforts to isolate Iran may also be pushing it deeper than at any point in recent years into China's economic orbit and closer to Russia's wartime strategic system," he said.

That does not mean China or Russia can make up for the economic cost of US pressure, since Iranian importers still need insurers, banks and customers able to absorb higher prices.

Container trains can only carry a fraction of what a large cargo vessel can transport, so for now, rail provides Iran with a limited alternative trade channel.

Its wider success depends on better financing and becoming more reliable — but even then it is not a wholesale substitute for sea freight.

#441. Raids at far-right group's offices at European Parliament

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Authorities searched the Patriots for Europe's European Parliament offices in a long-running funding inquiry. The group says the charges pertain to its predecessor and there is "no legal continuity" between the two.

The third-largest alliance of parties in the European Parliament, the right-wing nationalist Patriots for Europe (PfE) group, on Wednesday said searches had been carried out at "administrative offices" at the European Parliament buildings in Brussels and Strasbourg.

PfE said the searches appeared to be tied to the long-running probe into alleged misuse of funds by members of the now-disbanded Identity and Democracy (ID) Group.

PfE formed out of the ashes of ID, losing and gaining some members along the way, after the last European Parliament elections in 2024.

The European Public Prosecutor's Office — the independent body that investigates and prosecutes crimes against the EU budget, such as corruption or misuse of EU funds — said it searched premises in France and Belgium.

It said this was part of an ongoing investigation into the use of European Union funds by a former European Parliament group.

EPPO did not say which group was involved and said it would not publish further details for now for fear of compromising the investigation.

PfE, meanwhile, confirmed the activity around its offices but also tried to stress its distance from the case since its rebrand and reformation — even though its constituent political parties are largely the same with the notable exception of the Alternative for Germany (AfD) now being excluded.

"Since this morning, the European Public Prosecutor's Office has been carrying out searches in administrative offices at the European Parliament in Brussels and Strasbourg. It would appear that these searches are linked to the complaint lodged against the Identity and Democracy Group (ID) dissolved in 2024," it wrote in a statement.

"The Patriots for Europe Group emphasizes that there is no legal continuity with the ID Group and that it is not involved in the ongoing investigations," it said.

A French member of the European Parliament for the Rassemblement National or National Rally (RN) party, Jean-Paul Garraud, said that "the offices of staff members of the former Identity and Democracy group" were targeted.

European Parliament auditors concluded last year that ID members had misused at least €4.3 million (roughly $4.8 million) in EU funds during the previous legislative term from 2019 to 2024.

According to their report, the money flowed to companies close to the group and to associations supporting anti-abortion campaigners.

The case was reminiscent of, but separate from, the conviction against RN leader Marine Le Pen, herself a former MEP, that for a time looked set to stifle her bid for the French presidency.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The ID group existed for one term in the European Parliament, from 2019 to 2024. It included France's RN, Germany's AfD, Austria's Freedom Party (FPÖ) and Italy's Lega, among other members.

It fell apart after major internal friction during the 2024 European election campaign, not least because of dissatisfaction within the group with their lead candidate Maximilian Krah of the AfD over a number of issues — from controversial remarks about Nazi Germany through to allegations of his close allies being Russian and Chinese influence, with one of Krah's aides since convicted of spying for China.

RN, FPÖ and Lega are part of the new PfE group, which claims "no legal continuity" to the dissolved ID alliance, but the AfD was not invited to join the PfE.

PfE has 85 seats in the 719-seat European Parliament, making it the third-largest group in the chamber, but a rather distant third behind the center-right European People's Party (184) and the center-left Socialists and Democrats (135).

The European Parliament relies heavily on such groupings, which try to unite political parties from various member states with broadly comparable policy positions and political alignment.

The AfD set up a much smaller splinter far-right grouping called Europe of Sovereign Nations (ESN), with just 27 seats, 15 of them held by the AfD.

#408. Bulgaria: Concerns about security in the Black Sea dominate presidential election campaign

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Bulgaria's presidential election campaign has been shaped by recent drone attacks on ships in the Black Sea and the government's reduced support for Ukraine, making foreign policy a key factor in the first round.

Six months after Bulgaria's parliamentary election, the country is gearing up to choose its next president.

Although the role of president in Bulgaria is largely ceremonial, the election on October 25 — with a potential runoff on November 1 — will serve as a test for the government of former President Rumen Radev, whose party won a landslide victory in April, securing an unprecedented majority — the first in Bulgaria in almost three decades.

Although there are 27 candidates on the ballot, the race has narrowed to a duel between the incumbent president, Iliana Yotova, who served two terms as Radev's vice president and is backed by the prime minister, and former caretaker Prime Minister Andrey Gyurov, who is supported by opposition parties.

According to the Bulgarian Constitution, the Bulgarian president embodies national unity. He or she represents the country at international level (with a few exceptions), is the commander-in-chief of its armed forces, appoints members of the Supreme Judicial Council and various regulatory bodies, including caretaker governments, and has the power to veto legislation.

Hristo Panchugov, a political scientist at the New Bulgarian University in Sofia, argued that although the president's powers are formally limited, the office is particularly significant in the current context.

"For the first time in Bulgaria's modern democratic history, we have an absolutely uncontested majority that is not held together by any coalition, but rather by a single political party that faces an opposition that is completely falling apart," he told DW.

Panchugov went on to say that it would have been better if the campaign had focused more on "whether the president could serve as a counterbalance."

Opposition candidate Andrey Gyurov reiterated this point in an interview with DW: "We are seeing a concentration of power that always bears the marks of a slide toward autocracy... A president should know how to say no."

Iliana Yotova declined DW's request for an interview.

Initially, the presidential race was characterized by small rallies, a lack of televised debates and numerous personal attacks on the two leading candidates.

In recent weeks, however, following drone attacks on ships in the Black Sea, the two frontrunners' diverging positions on support for Ukraine have come to the fore.

Bulgaria's government initially declined invitations to join the Coalition of the Willing and NATO's anti-drone initiative but changed its position on the latter after the recent drone attacks.

Yotova has advocated for the establishment of a "Coalition of Peace," in which she believes Bulgaria would have a stronger diplomatic voice in the process to endRussia's war in Ukraine.

"There are many warmongers in our country; I am not one of them," she said at a campaign rally this month. "As long as I am president — and if you place your trust in me — not a single Bulgarian soldier will go to Ukraine."

Yotova has sought to reassure voters that no Bulgarian troops will be sent to Ukraine, even though this has not featured in public debate, and has been ruled out by a 2025 parliamentary declaration that reaffirmed that the Bulgarian Armed Forces would not participate in hostilities there.

Nevertheless, the recent drone attacks on commercial ships in the Black Sea have put new pressure on both the president and Rumen Radev's Progressive Bulgaria party.

"The drone strikes in the Black Sea brought the otherwise abstract geopolitical situation right into the daily lives of every Bulgarian," Boriana Dimitrova, managing partner of the Alpha Research market and social research agency, told DW.

Many in Europe, including Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy, have laid the blame for the attacks squarely at Russia's door. The fact that Bulgaria's leaders did not clearly attribute blame for the attacks fueled frustration across the country.

After the first drone attack, President Yotova convened the country's Consultative Council on National Security. Its subsequent declaration did not name the source of the threat in the Black Sea.

A week later, after the second attack, Prime Minister Radev said, "We lack solid, clear evidence to level accusations against any specific party; however logic suggests the outcome works to the advantage of Russia." He then summoned the Russian ambassador for talks.

The stance of the government and president is having an impact on the race.

"We're seeing a slight decline in support for Iliana Yotova, which is not threatening her first-place position by any means in the first round, but there has been a decline given the ambiguity of some of the positions she has taken," said Boriana Dimitrova.

As the issue of security entered the public debate, some Bulgarians took to the streets in Sofia to protest against what they saw as the government's ambiguous foreign policy stance on this matter.

"I think the drone attacks in the Black Sea and the utterly spineless stance of the president, the prime minister, and everyone acting in unison were the last straw, and I believe that, on the eve of the presidential election, this should serve as a catalyst," Vlada Tzenova, one of the protesters, told DW.

All this is reflected in the preliminary results of the latest opinion poll conducted by Alpha Research. While expected voter turnout is up, the gap between the two frontrunners has narrowed: Yotova is still ahead, leading Gyurov by about 12 percentage points in the first round, but the margin is smaller than it was.

Nevertheless, these recent dynamics do not necessarily mean a shift in the balance of power.

Panchugov said that Gyurov's campaign has been hampered by his hesitancy to declare his candidacy, a lack of "passion" in his campaign, and the fact that he made few comments on current affairs before he joined the race.

Moreover, supporters of the candidate of the pro-Russian nationalist Vazrazhdane (Revival) party, which ranked third in the Alpha Research poll, would be most likely to support Yotova in a second round.

What could decide the outcome of the election is the fact that both GERB and DPS, two of the established parties that attracted the support of more than 660,000 voters in April, did not field a presidential candidate.

According to Alpha Research, half of GERB's supporters do not intend to vote in the first round. The party dominated Bulgaria's political landscape for 17 years but lost power in December following mass anti-corruption protests backed by opposition parties supporting Andrey Gyurov.

Most of the remaining GERB supporters are expected to back Gyurov, as the party has historically opposed Iliana Yotova's political home, the Bulgarian Socialist Party.

At the same time, both Gyurov's campaign and nationalist parties are seeing increased mobilization, with the latter drawing support from voters disappointed by the government's cautious response to the drone attacks.

"We can view these elections as the first test of confidence in the party that recently won the election," Boriana Dimitrova said. "In peaceful times, the center attracts the most attention; in turbulent times, those candidates who take a clearer stance — regardless of the direction — gain greater visibility."

#388. Ukraine: Life near the front line in Sloviansk

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

As Russian continues to attack Sloviansk in eastern Ukraine, thousands of residents refuse to leave or struggle to find a way out. For many, poverty, family ties and beloved pets make evacuation painfully difficult.

A few days ago, three Russian aerial bombs struck a residential neighborhood on the outskirts of Sloviansk. One hit a five-story apartment building, injuring seven people. Along with neighboring Kramatorsk, Sloviansk is one of the last major towns in the eastern Ukrainian region of Donetsk still under Ukrainian control. Since summer, the city has been a combat zone.

In a courtyard surrounded by damaged apartment blocks, a dusty carpet hangs over the metal frame of a swing set. Tetjana, a pensioner who lives in the building across the street, has brought it out of her rubble-strewn apartment to clean it. She was in her kitchen when the bombs struck.

"The bomb hit the second floor, and a woman who lived on the fourth floor was screaming," she tells DW. "They managed to rescue her before that entire section of the building collapsed," she adds.

Despite being offered the opportunity to evacuate, Tetjana has yet to leave Sloviansk. "I don't know where I would go," she says. Rent can cost up to €400 ($449) a month. Tetjana's pension is the equivalent of just €67 ($75) a month. Even with monthly relocation assistance of about €40 ($45), she could not make ends meet. "I couldn't live on that. Nowhere!"

Meanwhile, electricity and water are still available in her neighborhood, but the gas supply has been cut off. "We'll get through this," Tetjana says hopefully. "Everything will be OK. That's what I tell everyone. I want that so much."

Days after the attack, the few residents remaining in the damaged buildings were still clearing out their apartments. But another attack hit as they worked. This time, the bombs struck a building housing a state-run emergency assistance center and the Red Cross. While firefighters put out the blaze, rescue workers and volunteers retrieved belongings left inside and cleared away broken glass. Eight people were injured in the attack.

"I was on the balcony when there was a deafening bang. It felt as if someone had hit me over the head," says Natalja, describing one of the recent attacks. The pensioner comes to this part of the city to care for her twin sister. "She can no longer get around. Today, I couldn't bring her bread, only crispbread," she tells DW. Although shops and a few stalls remain at the burned-out market, bread is not available every day.

"I tried to persuade my sister to leave. I would go to a nursing home and she would go to a hospital. But her dog is sick, and I have one, too," Natalja says, noting that pets are not allowed during evacuations. "I can't leave my sister behind. She is worried and angry with me, as if everything were my fault. I have no strength left."

Natalja slowly sets off for home, "so I can get there before dark." The city center is nearly 4 kilometers (2.5 miles) away. Public transportation has been suspended since 30 people were killed in an attack on two buses in neighboring Kramatorsk.

The center of Sloviansk is heavily damaged. Few people are out on the streets, though a handful of shops remain open. Of the city's 100,000 residents, about 20,000 remain, most of them elderly people who are unable to leave.

Vasyl, a pensioner, is from the village of Mayaky, north of Sloviansk. A neighbor drove him into the city center, where he sat down for lunch in a cafe. "We're used to living under fire by now," he says. "But it's a bad habit. We should have broken it long ago."

Vasyl wants peace for his homeland. But he rejects Russian President Vladimir Putin's demand that the part of the Donetsk region under Ukrainian control be handed over to Moscow.

Two elderly women talk on a street covered with nets to protect against drones. "I can't remember the last time I slept through the night," says Iryna, a pensioner. "It's terrible, but I have nowhere to go. I'm supposed to leave my two cats behind, but I love them so much. They're all I have."

"We can only hide, trembling, and pray to God that everything will be all right," Iryna continues. "When I have to go to the market, I don't know if I'll make it there alive."

Russian drones attacked the central market several weeks ago, injuring at least three people. Since then, fewer vendors and customers have been coming to the market.

Valeriya sells women's underwear. With kindergartens closed, she has to bring her 5-year-old daughter Sofia to work. Their neighborhood in Sloviansk is not yet subject to the mandatory evacuation of families with children.

"We're used to this life by now. We were evacuated in 2022, but later returned. Now we're waiting to be evacuated again," Valeriya says.

Sofia races through the market aisles with her friend Anya, who is a year older. Anya's mother is a soldier defending her hometown. Her grandmother, Tetjana, sits nearby selling men's clothing. She, too, has to look after her granddaughter while working.

Tetjana has started shipping her belongings to the Dnipropetrovsk region, where she hopes to start a new life. She is sending them while the post office is still open. "It hurts to see our Ukraine being destroyed," she says, fighting back tears. "Who will rebuild our cities?"

She hopes to return one day. But the fate of Bakhmut weighs on her mind. "Look at Bakhmut. There are no houses left. I'm counting on our boys to hold our cities, even though it's very difficult." Bakhmut, about 50 kilometers (31 miles) from Sloviansk, fell to Russian forces in 2023 after months of heavy fighting.

The women see little hope for a ceasefire. "Putin won't want one. And our younger generation will ask us what we fought for in the first place," Tetjana says. She believes Russia must withdraw from Ukrainian territory.

Drones buzz overhead as gunfire echoes across the market. "It's hard for our boys to shoot them down," Tetjana says. "But they're doing their best."

#339. Germany sit out FIFA Under-15 World Cup as Russia return

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Germany is one a handful of FIFA's 211 nations to refuse an invitation to play at the inaugural Under-15 World Cup. The tournament will also mark Russia's return to the FIFA fold.

With nearly 1,000 matches and 189 teams, the Under-15 World Cup in Azerbaijan is bloated even by FIFA's usual standards. It marks a change in its attitude to children's international tournaments, which have not existed on anywhere near this scale before.

For Germany, it is too much too soon. The German FA (DFB) told DW that it had "consciously decided not to participate." The statement from the organization suggested the DFB — whose development of young players is under scrutiny after a run of poor performances by the men's national team — felt such a tournament was not appropriate for players so young.

"At this age level, our identification and development process is still underway, and we therefore do not believe it is appropriate to nominate a fixed squad for an international tournament at this stage," it read.

"More broadly, the DFB's approach to talent development is based on the principle of supporting young players in a long-term and age-appropriate manner. We carefully assess at which stage international competitions and development measures are beneficial for each age group.

"In our view, young talents should not be exposed too early to the accompanying aspects of professional football. These include increased expectations and pressure, public evaluation of their performances, and early interest from agents and the football market."

The refusal to play in the tournament, which began this week, is consistent with Germany's more cautious approach to youth development. While in some leagues, like the Premier League in England, there is no regulation about youths playing professionally, in Germany's top flight, the Bundesliga, players must be 16. In fact, before the 2020-21 season, players could not be granted a pro license before they were 17 years and six months old.

That change enabled Youssoufa Moukoko to make his Bundesliga debut for Borussia Dortmund a day after his 16th birthday in 2020, becoming the league's youngest player in the process.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

But, some would argue his is a cautionary tale. Hyped wildly due to his goalscoring exploits at youth level, Moukoko was fast-tracked by Dortmund and Germany, who called him up for the 2022 World Cup. Now, still just 21, he is struggling for game time and goals on loan at Corum, in the Turkish Super League, from Danish side Copenhagen.

Players under 16 are also forbidden by FIFA from having an agent. But this is poorly regulated in some regions of the world. That led Bundesliga club St. Pauli, then in the second division, to announce they would no longer work with player agents or private agencies in their youth system in 2023.

"We want to focus on partnership and dialogue with the players, their families and their relatives," explained the head of the club's youth academy, Benjamin Liedtke, as St. Pauli officially positioned themselves "against the commercialization of youth football."

Germany are one of 22 nations to refuse the invitation to compete in the tournament, 18 of whom are from UEFA, Europe's confederation. The USA are also one of the prominent refusals alongside Ukraine, whose FA told German public broadcaster Sportschau that they initially refused on sporting grounds before claiming that "other countries indicated that they would compete but did not want to play against Russia." DW contacted a number of FAs to clarify this point but is yet to receive a response.

FIFA President Gianni Infantino, who has become a notorious figure thanks to a number of scandals over the past year, told Sky in February: "We have to [look at reinstating Russia], definitely, because this ban has not achieved anything. It has just created frustration and hatred. Having girls and boys from Russia being able to play football games in other parts of Europe would help."

Eight months later, he has his wish. The ongoing tournament, billed as the "FIFA U-15 World Cup & Festival" comprises eight-a-side games with 20 minute halves, a smaller pitch and a number of other technical tweaks. FIFA say the 2027 edition will be for girls teams only.

"Young players need meaningful matches, different opponents and different football experiences in order to develop. Talent exists everywhere, but the opportunities available to young players are not always the same," said FIFA Chief of Global Football Development, Arsene Wenger, likely referencing that teams from Bhutan and Seychelles to the Bahamas and Vanuatu are present.

"This tournament gives players from across the world the chance to compete, to learn and to grow."

FIFA are yet to respond to a DW request for further comment.

The round robin stage, according to FIFA, has placed teams in "groups that provide diverse sporting and regional representation" but also appears to have, at least initially, kept apart nations in conflict with each other.

For example, Israel and Palestine or Kosovo and Serbia are not in the same groups but could, in theory, meet in the later stages of the tournament. This would not be allowed in senior football, thanks to various bans and workarounds.

FIFA are vague about how the tournament works after the round robin, saying only that it will consist of "four to five additional matches per team, determined by results and subsequent tier placement. [There will be] one winner for each of the six tiers." It also said that the winner of the top tier will be crowned champions but all teams will play matches until the tournament concludes on October 30.

But, given that they have been banned from international football since invading Ukraine in 2022, it is the presence of a Russian national team at a FIFA tournament that will likely heap the most controversy on to young shoulders. Though a Russian presence was not the reason for German refusal, not attending for sporting reasons may also turn out to be a smart piece of sport politics.

#313. Ukraine tries to shut Moscow's airports with drone strikes on Russia

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Repeated Ukrainian drone attacks are increasingly disrupting operations at Moscow's airports. The pounding is part of Ukraine's plan to shut down Russia's airpace.

For months, drone strikes by Ukraine have forced the suspension of flights to and from airports in Russia. Moscow's Domodedovo, Sheremetyevo, Zhukovsky and Vnukovo airports have had to shut down operations almost daily in October, according to reports from the independent investigative media outlet Agentstvo. On some days, the airports were reportedly closed for up to nine hours.

In early September, President Volodymyr Zelenskyy announced that Ukraine would seek to cut off the airspace around Moscow and other destinations in Russia. "Today, we would like to warn every airline that uses Russian airspace, every insurer, and everyone who continues to use major Russian airports: Russian airspace will become completely unsafe," Zelenskyy said.

The following day, Ukraine sent a corresponding warning to the International Civil Aviation Organization.

Ukraine's airspace has been closed since the start of Russia's full-scale invasion in February 2022.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Russia's aviation authority, Rosaviatsiya, has issued an emergency safety "carpet" protocol that is designed to immediately shut down the airspace over a specific region or around affected airports if an aircraft violates Russian airspace, weapons are used, or unidentified objects appear in the skies. Once the protocol is announced, drastic restrictions apply to all air traffic.

"If there is a military threat, a specific section of airspace is closed off, and this carpet is declared," Oleksandr Laneckij, the chief executive officer of the Friendly Avia Support consultancy, told DW. "Essentially, every drone poses a military threat."

Laneckij noted that Yemen's Houthis have managed to significantly disrupt Saudi airspace. "I think Ukraine's military capabilities far exceed those of the Houthis," Laneckij said.

The military analyst Jan Matvejev also said Ukraine would have little trouble severely disrupting Moscow-area air traffic. Though Ukraine has not yet managed to completely shut down Russian airspace as Zelenskyy plans, Matvejev said, its armed forces "remain committed to this idea."

"To close off the airspace and trigger the 'carpet' plan, all you need are simple drones that don't have to be equipped with a warhead or even have a long range," Matvejev said. Ukraine's arms industry can produce hundreds of combat-ready long-range drones in a day, he added. That is why he believes Ukraine could have the necessary resources to shut down Moscow's airports.

Indeed, Ukraine's efforts to shut down Russian airspace are "just getting started," Simon Shuster wrote in October in the US magazine The Atlantic, citing well-informed sources.

Matvejev said Russia's air-defense systems were not entirely effective in efforts to prevent Ukrainian drones from penetrating Moscow's airspace. "If it were possible to shoot them down, they would have done so by now," he said. He added that Russia would have deployed additional air-defense troops if they were available.

Laneckij said Russia might lower the threshold for declaring the carpet protocol, which could increase the risk of deadly incidents such as the 2024 Christmas day crash of an Azerbaijan Airlines flight. Investigators say the flight was struck by a Russian air-defense missile that was being used to defend against drones, killing 38 people.

Matvejev seconded the fear that Russia's defenses could again mistakenly target civilian aircraft instead of military objects. He said little had changed about Russia's air-defense protocol since the Azeri flight was shot in 2024.

In an operation against a low-flying drone, the target is visually identified and fired upon with a machine gun. In such a scenario, the danger to aircraft is extremely low, unless they happen to be flying in the immediate vicinity.

The risk to civil aviation increases significantly when anti-aircraft missiles are deployed, Matvejev said. A missile could have defects that are practically unavoidable in mass production and could veer toward an aircraft flying nearby. Matvejev said such defense measures could not guarantee the safety of civilian aircraft during operations.

He said drones did not have to carry explosives to be disruptive. A collision between an airplane and a basic drone can have serious consequences — and, he said, a drone sent from Ukraine all the way to Moscow must be relatively large, "weighting dozens of kilograms."

In the summer of 2023, Ukrainian armed forces attacked the airport in the Russian city of Pskov, where both military and civilian aircraft were parked. Earlier this month, drone debris fell on the Sochi airport, on Russia's Black Sea coast.

Russia also attacks Ukraine's airports. In September, Zelenskyy said Kyiv's airport had been struck, as well as the Boryspil airport near the capital.

Laneckij said three factors would determine whether international airlines suspend their flights to Russia: the military threat, the degree of demand and the insurance cost.

He said issues could emerge around insurance. When insurers rate the security risk of flying to Russia as very high, they could significantly raise their rates for aircraft headed there. This would cause ticket prices to skyrocket.

Competition within Russia's aviation market has already declined because many airlines are no longer in business, Laneckij said. This has increased demand for tickets from the remaining foreign airlines.

"Whether they stay or not depends on the price they have to pay for the danger faced," he said.

#293. EU moves to loosen rules on pesticide use as experts sound the alarm

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The European Union wants to relax rules for pesticides, saying it will be easier for farmers to adopt more environmentally friendly options. But health and environmental experts say the risks are far too great.

As part of its drive to "streamline and simplify" EU laws, the European Commission has proposed sweeping changes for agriculture and food safety legislation. These include a move to ease rules controlling the use of pesticides and herbicides, which help fight pests and weeds.

Under current EU rules, which date back to 2011, primary pesticide components, or active substances, are only granted approval for limited periods of no more than 15 years.

The European Commission's proposed revamp of the system would make indefinite greenlighting — including of the controversial glyphosate, which has been linked to cancer and kills almost all plant life — the default. Safety reviews would only be done in exceptional cases, and regulatory authorities, not the industry, would bear responsibility.

The commission has said its goal is to cut costs, simplify work for authorities and help farmers stay competitive. Scientists and doctors, however, warn that concerns over human health and environmental impact from these toxic substances are being brushed aside.

The European Commission has said it's responding to complaints from farmers and the agriculture industry to clarify EU rules that govern pesticide use.

A joint statement released in May by 19 food associations, including CropLife Europe, which promotes pesticides, said complicated administrative procedures and a lengthy approvals process meant farmers were "left without any effective solutions to protect their crops against insects, weeds and diseases," putting food security and livelihoods at risk.

"The existing system of periodic renewal assessment of every active substance has become unsustainable," the commission's press service told DW, citing "chronic delays" and "bottlenecks" in the approval of new, more sustainable products.

In recent negotiations, EU member states said open-ended authorizations should be restricted to "low-risk active substances."

But their stance is still wanting to extend initial approval periods for other substances to up to 15 years, and a maximum of 25 years for renewals.

Herbert Dorfmann, a center-right Italian member of the European Parliament, said there is currently a yearslong delay in approving new pesticides, including more eco-friendly options, meaning farmers are forced to rely on older substances. Or, if these are banned, go without altogether.

The commission wants to "make the system more efficient and responsive," while guaranteeing that "the protection of human and animal health and the environment remain the highest priorities."

Dorfmann, who as co-rapporteur is responsible for interinstitutional negotiations on the issue, backs the commission's stance, saying the EU needs "to open up the system for innovation."

He aims to bring the parliament's proposal on the new pesticide rules to a vote among EU lawmakers before the end of the year.

Ecologists and medical experts, however, have spoken out forcefully against the proposed changes, saying the potential implications for human health and the environment are being downplayed.

In September, more than 500 experts from across the EU signed an open letter calling on the European Parliament to suspend its consideration of the new regulations and conduct an independent investigation into health and environmental impacts.

On October 7, UN rights experts also backed the call for a full review into whether the proposed changes violated the EU's obligations under international human rights law, especially with regard to agricultural workers and children.

Pesticides can persist in the environment, contaminating water and degrading soils. They can also drive biodiversity loss and poison other animals higher up the food chain.

When it comes to humans, the European Environment Agency has said it was "not yet possible to estimate the burden of disease linked to current levels of exposure to all pesticides."

However, exposure to chemical pesticides, including banned substances that still persist in the environment, has been linked to various cancers, chronic respiratory disease, neurological disorders and developmental delays, as well as problems with fertility and behavior.

And exposure is widespread: A survey conducted between 2014 and 2021 found that 84% of urine samples from both children and adults in five European countries contained residue from two or more pesticides.

Martina Vijver, scientific director and ecotoxicology expert at Leiden University in the Netherlands, said experts were "deeply concerned" that the EU's current plan "will weaken rather than strengthen the level of protection for pesticide regulation."

Speaking at the European Parliament in September, she said regular reassessments were key to evaluating the safety of pesticides with new findings, pointing out that the perception of risk changes over time.

"The regulatory system must remain capable of correcting yesterday's decisions with tomorrow's knowledge," she said.

Salome Roynel, a policy officer at the Pesticide Action Network Europe, an NGO based in Brussels, said the harmful effects of pesticides aren't often fully understood until years later, when the product is up for reconsideration.

"New science emerges all the time," she told DW, highlighting how 56 active substances have been banned in the EU since 2011 as a result of periodic reviews.

That includes the insecticide chlorpyrifos, previously used in orchards and on vegetable and cereal crops, which has been tied to harmful effects on children's brain development.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Roynel said it was "obvious that there was some kind of alignment" between the changes proposed by the EU institutions and the demands of the pesticide lobby.

This echoes a new report by nonprofit research and campaign group Corporate Europe Observatory, which said Dorfmann and other lawmakers had "copy-pasted […] industry demands" into their regulatory proposals.

Dorfmann denied the lobby influence, telling DW he had met with "both sides, industry and environmental associations," in public debates on the issue.

"The main objective is not to keep old products in the market. The main objective is to bring new ones to the market," Dorfmann said.

Part of that plan, he added, would be to help more environmentally friendly "biocontrol" products and low-risk substances to bypass current administrative hurdles and get on the market faster.

It's an idea previously championed by Vijver, who agrees that the current system means more environmentally friendly pesticides are stuck in an administrative "traffic jam." She told DW that biocontrol products could go in a green "bus lane" to ease the way to approval at the European Food Safety Authority, while maintaining crucial safety reviews to filter out the more dangerous products.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Roynel also supports the plan to speed up approval for biocontrol substances, but said it needs to be part of a wider system change that includes financial support, monitoring and training to use these new products, as well as maintaining the restrictions on more harmful chemical pesticides ― not further deregulation.

"If farmers get easier access to synthetic pesticides tomorrow, they would have no incentive to switch to biocontrol," she said. "It's important to put [biocontrol pesticides] on the market and we fully support that, but… it will not be the silver bullet."

#251. France: Hundreds arrested after nationwide protests

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The French government says more than 1,000 people were arrested following nationwide protests on Saturday. Police and elite security forces were out in force across the country as clashes broke out.

This blog is now closed. Thank you for reading.

You can read all our updates on the protests and unrest in France and other parts of Europe on October 24 and 25, 2026, below.

Marine Le Pen launched her fourth bid to become president of France on Sunday with a plea to "French patriots" to back her and her far-right National Rally (RN) in spring elections.

The grande dame of the far-right, Le Pen delivered an impassioned invocation of French spirit in Orleans, calling for "a renaissance" of the country's virtues and stressing its rich creative and intellectual history.

She blasted "Little Marquis Macron" and the state of France under his stewardship — both domestically and internationally — offering a fiery outline of the need to restore order, but also civility in the country, alongside promises of maintaining French (nuclear) defense and judicial sovereignty.

Le Pen said "we'll protect our public spaces so that they can remain places full of life and mutual respect" and cited civility as the surest way to keep violence out of society.

She praised regionalism over globalism or supranationalism in the form of the European Union (EU), and spoke of the need to defend freedom and the "French way of life," making clear that, "In France, you live like the French."

Le Pen recalled General Charles de Gaulle several times throughout her speech, emphasizing the historic importance of the moment when it comes to securing the promise of the future.

After ending her remarks with a full-throated "Vive la France!," a relieved-looking Le Pen was joined on stage by heir apparent Jordan Bardella and party members for a rousing rendition of the national anthem under the campaign slogan, "Pour la France La Renaissance."

Amid the latest demonstrations, what's driving the unrest, and could a broader youth-led protest movement be taking shape?

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

French unions and protest groups have called for fresh demonstrations on October 31 and a nationwide strike on November 5 to maintain pressure on the government.

The left-wing Solidaires union federation said anger over wages, living costs and public services was bringing together students, workers, firefighters and other groups.

"We see an aggregation of anger," Solidaires co-leader Murielle Guilbert told broadcaster BFM TV.

The SUD Education teaching union said it would join the October 31 demonstrations. The larger CGT union is set to decide during the week.

The Yellow Vests movement also called for rallies, including sit-ins outside town halls and the National Assembly.

The government has opened talks with unions and student representatives, but protest leaders say its proposals are inadequate.

French authorities detained 1,314 people during nationwide protests on Saturday, according to a provisional Interior Ministry tally.

The ministry said 53 police officers and other security personnel were injured, firefighters responded to 75 fires and 24 businesses were looted. It did not provide a figure for injured protesters.

Clashes broke out in cities including Paris, Marseille, Bordeaux, Toulouse and Tours, with police using tear gas. The ministry blamed groups including radical far-left activists for confrontations along or near protest routes.

Authorities counted 410,000 participants at 289 demonstrations and gatherings, down from 550,000 a week earlier. Other estimates put Saturday's turnout as high as 800,000.

The reported arrests and violence were substantially lower than on October 17, when authorities recorded 2,628 detentions and 1,144 injured security personnel.

The ministry said several investigations had been opened and described the subsequent night as calm.

With Saturday's protest over, French Interior Minister Laurent Nunez reported nearly 1,000 arrests.

The number is expected to rise as security officials tally up the day's events, Nunez added.

"Multiple lootings have been averted," he said.

While some clashes between protesters and police were reported in many French cities, the scope of the unrest was much smaller compared to the protests last Saturday, which saw buildings defaced and burned, and some 1,500 shops looted across the country.

Nunez previously put the number of people arrested last weekend at 2,700.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Some 410,000 people marched across France on Saturday, according to the country's interior ministry, as the country braced for a possible repeat of violence seen last weekend.

In Paris alone, at least 110,000 ‌people turned out to denounce rising ⁠living ⁠costs and growing inequality.

Police were out in force in major French cities. While rallies in Paris and Marseille were mostly peaceful, clashes between protesters and police were reported in towards the evening.

Riot police in Paris fired tear gas and water cannons at some 500 hooded youths before they eventually dispersed. In Marseille, youths blocked roads by setting trash cans on fire. They threw rocks and bottles at security forces.

Clashes were also reported in the cities of Lyon, Bordeaux, Grenoble, Clermont-Ferrand, Lille, Rennes, Nantes and Toulouse.

France is known for its protest culture, where demonstration is considered a bedrock value.

Altercations between the police and demonstrators are by and large part of the French protest ritual.

What we are seeing could even be considered a relatively calm end-of-demonstration situation, Lisa Louis, who is in Paris, said.

Meanwhile, security guards were deployed in Milan on Saturday amid demonstrations against the rising cost of living.

Italian Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni ordered 500 soldiers to be dispatched to the city, in addition to about 5,000 police and other law enforcement personnel there.

But the protests in the large Italian city were less unruly than last weekend, when looters ransacked businesses near the central Duomo area. Around 3,000 people rallied in Milan, with another smaller demonstration taking place in Rome.

As the day went on (and largely toward the end of demonstrations in many cities), clashes occurred between protesters and the authorities during the demonstrations across France.

The French interior ministry said it estimates 410,000 people to have taken part in protest throughout France today.

Demonstrations were set to take place in Paris and Marseille but protesters took to the streets in other cities for rallies like Lyon and Nantes.

There were reports about clashes between protesters and police in several cities, including Paris.

There were protests in the city of Lyon, in the east-central part of France, where police used tear gas to disperse protesters wearing masks.

There are also reports about clashes in Marseille, where police fired tear gas to disperse crowds.

About 550,000 people attended demonstrations throughout the country last weekend.

The mood over the past few days in France felt almost a bit apocalyptic. Business owners told me they were worried more looting might lie ahead, after gangs of individuals had ransacked and destroyed at least 3,000 shops across the country last weekend.

"Some of us are planning to buy weapons to defend ourselves, in case the pillagers come back. One day, there's going to be a tragedy — for sure," Jonathan Siad, owner of a tobacco shop in Paris, said to me.

Other store owners were too afraid to talk to us in front of the camera – they worried criminals might see the interview and target their shop.

Residents told me they would try to avoid the area where the demonstration was taking place. Only to add that they hoped the situation wouldn't escalate like last week, when even places far away from the actual marches became hotspots of clashes between the police and protesters.

Interior Minister Laurent Nunez had admitted on Monday a "loss of control" last Saturday. The authorities thus increased the number of police deployed across France by 10,000 to 70,000 this Saturday.

First clashes between protesters and the police started to occur in the late afternoon in several cities across France such as Marseille, Lyon and Paris.

But for now, the much feared mass looting and ransacking doesn't seem to be happening. The situation is nowhere near the total chaos Paris and other cities like Marseille experienced last Saturday.

"Last weekend, our neighborhood resembled a war zone with gangs roaming the area. Today, it's completely empty apart from police with their vans," Yassaman Metghalchi, co-owner of a jewelry shop in the city center of Paris that got looted last Saturday, told me.

"All of our neighbors feel reassured – although the night is still young and we need to wait and see what will happen," she added.

Paris police say 110,000 protesters were on the streets in the city on Saturday. The anti-government "Yellow Vest" movementteamed up with aggrieved school students for this round of demonstrations.

The CGT trade union, meanwhile, said 800,000 participated in protests across France, with 250,000 people taking part in Paris.

Saturday's protesters were limited by Parisian authorities to the city's southeastern edge, away from the center.

Authorities prepared to help the protesters march safely from the Place d'Italie in the 13th arrondissement on the left bank of the Seine River to Porte de Vincennes which lies further away on the right bank.

A post on X by Paris Police Prefecture showed the Porte d'Italie from the sky ahead of the march.

French news agency AFP reported clashes in Paris after the demonstrations.

The French police used tear gas in response to protests in the southeastern city of Lyon. Use of tear gas was also reported in the cities of Lille, Toulouse and Nantes.

In Orleans, south of Paris, protesters also came out to the streets against a two-day congress held in the city of Marine Le Pen's far-right National Rally party.

According to polls, Le Pen is the frontrunner in France's presidential elections next year.

#229. Steinmeier in China: A major state visit in uncertain times

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

President Frank-Walter Steinmeier is traveling to China on a state visit in a period of political and economic uncertainty. Germany is seeking alternative partners in the region but also wants good ties with Beijing.

Eight years have passed since Frank-Walter Steinmeier's last trip to China. Since then, the relationship between China and Europe has changed significantly. The German president plans to spend several days touring China before concluding his visit in Beijing, where he will meet with China's leaders.

His visit comes just as the Chinese Communist Party's Central Committee is convening for its plenary session, a major annual event that foreign dignitaries usually avoid when timing their visits.

The focus of the president's five-day trip will be "Germany's diverse and complex relations with China, as well as the challenges involved," his office said in a statement. In addition to sites of cultural exchange, the itinerary will also include a visit to a Volkswagen plant in Hefei.

The automotive industry, in particular, illustrates the dilemma facing Germany's China policy. China is one of the most important markets for German automotive and machinery manufacturers, as well as for the chemical industry. At the same time, state-supported overcapacity in China is putting pressure on European manufacturers.

Not only that. Germany and Europe are dependent on China for raw materials and technologies, which means they are increasingly seeking out alternative partnerships.

Steinmeier was once an advocate of the policy of "change through trade" — the idea that other countries could be persuaded to become more democratic with the leverage of global trade — which has few adherents left in Germany today.

Now he must now pursue a tougher European line in Beijing. Caught between partnership and competition, Europe leaders recently managed to reach an agreement: Imports of plug-in hybrid vehicles from China are to be scaled back.

"That was a concrete step, but it can only be the beginning," said Michael Laha, an expert on China at the German Council on Foreign Relations (DGAP) in Berlin.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Coinciding with Steinmeier's state visit to China, Chancellor Friedrich Merz is traveling to the Indo-Pacific with business representatives. His visit to Japan, Singapore and South Korea will focus on competitiveness, technological upheavals, defense cooperation and critical raw materials. Merz is visiting companies and attending important regional economic conferences in Tokyo and Seoul.

The dual trips are Berlin's attempt to send a twofold message: While the chancellor is "deliberately strengthening Germany's partnerships in the Indo-Pacific at a time of global upheaval," according to Laha, the president is seeking dialogue with Beijing at a time when Germany and Europe are recalibrating their relationship with China.

"This joint trip sends a very well-coordinated message to the Chinese that we remain committed to the partnership but at the same time are taking steps to reduce risk," said Laha, before adding that this is a difficult balancing act that also allows China to save face.

While Merz will be accompanied by a large economic delegation, Steinmeier will be traveling to China with representatives from German culture, politics, and academia. Mikko Huotari, director of the China Institute MERICS, is also expected to be part of the delegation — even though Beijing has imposed sanctions on the institute. "The fact that he is coming along sends a clear message to the Chinese side that we do not approve of the sanctions," said Laha.

In 2021, the EU imposed sanctions against several Chinese party and regional officials, as well as an organization accused of serious human rights violations and oppressing the Uyghur minority. It was the first time such sanctions had been imposed on China since the Tiananmen Square massacre in 1989, and Beijing did not take kindly to them. In response, the Chinese government imposed entry bans and other sanctions against European politicians, scholars, and institutions, including MERICS.

Laha did not expect human rights to play a major role in the German president's trip. Steinmeier is considered a pragmatist when it comes to China policy. As foreign minister, he distanced himself from his predecessor Joschka Fischer, a critic of China, and even criticized Chancellor Angela Merkel when she hosted the Dalai Lama.

On the subject of Ukraine, Steinmeier is likely to be more direct and raise the issue of China's role in the conflict. "Beijing is clearly on Moscow's side," said Laha. In Berlin, however, officials are convinced that China can do more to persuade Russia to engage in serious negotiations and find a solution to the conflict.

Steinmeier may make this clear in his speech at Peking University on Thursday. Germany has "long viewed China's emergence with respect and goodwill," read a statement from the president's office. Today, however, the focus is less on China's continued rise and more on how it uses its growing power.

"If the president points out that Russia used its power to attack a sovereign state, he could be alluding to another regional connection: Taiwan," said Laha. That could be seen as an appeal to Beijing to assume more responsibility for the regional and international order.

To conclude his visit, Steinmeier will meet with China's leadership in Beijing. Talks are planned with President Xi Jinping, whom he has known for nearly two decades, as well as with Premier Li Qiang and the chairman of the National People's Congress, Zhao Leji.

The trip marks the culmination of Steinmeier's focus on Asia during his second term in office. Since then, he has deliberately sought to forge close ties with partners in the region and has advocated for greater diversification. Now China, which is Germany's most important and most challenging partner, will come to its own conclusion.

This article was originally published in German.

#219. What Germany thinks of carmaker Audi's $4 billion deal with Trump

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The luxury carmaker is set to open its first factory in the United States — a move Trump credits to his tariffs. DW examines the German reaction amid a massive auto industry cull at home.

US President Donald Trump announced the investment on Saturday, during a campaign rally in Georgia, a key battleground state for his Republican Party in the midterm elections next week.

Audi quickly confirmed the plan, saying it would build its first US factory near Macon, Georgia, costing around $4 billion (€3.6 billion) and creating some 4,500 jobs.

The German automaker said construction would begin next year, with a planned annual production of up to 200,000 vehicles due to start in the early 2030s.

The firm plans to build premium SUVs for the US market at the plant, marking a "long-term commitment" to what it said was "one of the world's most important premium markets."

The US investment comes as German automakers face unprecedented pressure from China, rising costs and two conflicts.

Backed by huge government subsidies, Chinese rivals have stolen a march in the transition to electric vehicles (EVs), scaling up production of new models that are fast gaining market share in a crowded global auto market.

BMW, Mercedes-Benz and Audi's parent company, Volkswagen— the world's second-largest automaker — have all issued profit warnings in recent months as margins come under pressure.

They've also announced major cost-cutting. VW is preparing to axe up to 100,000 jobs by 2030, while Audi's factory in Neckarsulm, which has around 15,000 staff, is one of four plants at risk of closure.

Trump credited his 15% tariffs on European-made vehicles for Audi's investment, saying it "would not have happened without tariffs."

While the commitment does feed into Trump's plan to bring manufacturing back to the United States from abroad, Audi said it was making the huge outlay for growth and resilience.

“We have much greater ambitions for Audi in America," said the carmaker's CEO Gernot Döllner, who appeared alongside Trump at the Republican's campaign rally.

"The United States is one of the world's most important premium markets, and we have not yet realized our full potential. We are determined to change that ... This is about building a stronger Audi business in America — not only about building a factory," he said.

However, Trump's tariffs have undoubtedly added to the automaker's woes.

Audi, which currently exports to the US market from Europe and Mexico, said the additional costs amounted to about €1.2 billion ($1.3 billion) last year.

With more than 160,000 vehicles sold, Audi's US sales last year fell 16%, amid intense competition and after Trump scrapped subsidies on electric vehicles in its second-biggest market.

By building a US factory, Audi should be able to avoid or reduce Trump's tariffs, industry analysts say.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

There's been fierce criticism of the deal due to the severe pressure facing German automakers and because the new US investment could mean further job losses at home.

Much of the criticism focused on Döllner's rally appearance alongside Trump.

The center-left Social Democratic Party (SPD)'s transport policy spokesperson Isabel Cademartori described it as campaign assistance for an autocrat who is waging a trade war against Germany.

"This is a disgrace, especially considering the history of the Volkswagen Group," she stated, referring to the company's links with Adolf Hitler's Nazi party during World War II.

Christiane Benner, president of the powerful IG Metall union, labeled the rally appearance as "extremely embarrassing, politically unacceptable and not worthy of the company."

VW works council chief Daniela Cavallo went further, saying Döllner had let himself be turned "into a puppet in [Trump's] propaganda show."

Cavallo, however, noted that the US plant could make sense from a business perspective, as VW needs to grow its market share there.

An Audi spokesperson defended the US investment, saying it was "not a decision against Germany, but rather a step toward strengthening our global competitiveness."

Manfred Döss, chairman of Audi's supervisory board, insisted the automaker's future cannot be secured by cost-cutting and efficiency alone.

"We need the courage to invest, to seize new opportunities and to make decisions that will shape our company for decades to come," he added.

The announcement certainly appears timed to coincide with the US midterm elections on November 3.

Voters will decide whether the Republicans or Democrats hold the balance of power in Congress during Trump's last two years in office.

Holding the announcement in the key battleground state of Georgia is seen as a strategic move to boost the fortunes of local Republican candidates.

Since he returned to the White House last year, Trump has used tariffs and political pressure to force global automakers to relocate their factories to American soil.

Audi's investment follows moves by Stellantis and Japan's Toyota to expand US production, while Honda is in talks to build a new US plant.

US auto giants General Motors and Ford have also promised to increase domestic production.

While one investment announcement may not be enough to sway many voters, it serves one of Trump's main election promises — to revive industrial production in the US.

#210. UN sounds the alarm over Israel's West Bank military operation

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The UN has sounded the alarm on Israeli action in the West Bank city of Hebron after hundreds of families were displaced over the weekend. Israel has ordered the military to demolish homes after soldiers were killed.

The Israeli military operation in the occupied West Bank city of Hebron was met with sharp criticism from the head of the UN as well as the UN's human rights commissioner in comments made on Monday.

The criticism comes after an Israeli military operation was launched in the occupied West Bank in response to a shootout on Friday that left two Israeli soldiers and two Palestinian gunmen dead. Three more Israeli soldiers were also injured.

Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu ordered the military to demolish buildings in the West Bank city of Hebron and to close off the West Bank. It came just days before Israel's general election on Tuesday.

The United Nations Secretary General Antonio Guterres said on Monday that "settlements are expanding, evictions are unrelenting with Palestinians driven from their lands," in a post on X.

"We are seeing an increasingly coercive environment, with the spectre of ethnic cleaning looming ever larger," he wrote.

The UN Human Rights High Commissioner Volker Türk also issued a statement condemning Israeli actions, saying the "aggressive operation" has resulted in the forcible displacement of thousands of Palestinians, "demolished homes and commercial property, and deprived residents of the ability to move freely."

"Collective punishment is a grave violation of international law. Threats to empty and erase entire neighbourhoods also raise serious concerns of ethnic cleansing," he said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

A UN report, also released on Monday, that accuses Israel of committing war crimes in the West Bank, was rejected by the Israeli Foreign Ministry as "propaganda."

The ministry said that the findings were "not a human rights report but a propaganda document detached from reality, aimed at undermining Israel's right to self-defense, delegitimizing the Jewish state, and legitimizing jihadist terrorism against its citizens."

Israeli troops entered the Deir Baha neighborhood, where the assailants lived, and ordered residents to pack up and leave.

The Hebron municipality said that more than 3,000 people, including 200 babies, were ordered to leave.

The Israeli military said at the weekend that its troops were carrying out precise, targeted and thorough searches to "locate and dismantle terrorist infrastructure and locate weapons, alongside questioning suspects" in Hebron.

The military said the operation was expected to continue "for several days" and would focus on Deir Baha.

The largescale response to the firefight in the West Bank comes in the run-up to elections in Israel on Tuesday that could bring an end to Netanyahu's premiership.

Netanyahu has frequently painted himself as the only person who can keep Israel safe but his opponents have accused him of lacking a plan.
