#3410. Where Will Jazz Pharmaceuticals Be in 5 Years? Oncology Is Becoming Its Growth Engine

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Could Jazz Pharmaceuticals (JAZZ +0.53%) turn over a new leaf? Over the past five years, the company has faced various challenges that have contributed to unimpressive top-line growth. But the drugmaker is increasingly pushing into oncology, which has helped improve its financial results. Does that make Jazz Pharmaceuticals an attractive stock to own through the next five years? Let's find out how the company might perform through 2031.

In the second quarter, Jazz Pharmaceuticals' revenue grew by 16% year over year to $1.2 billion. That was a fairly strong performance for the company, and several products contributed to that. For instance, Jazz's Xywav, a medicine approved to treat cataplexy in narcolepsy patients, posted sales of $471.2 million, up 13.5% year over year. Xywav has done a good job of replacing Jazz Pharmaceuticals' Xyrem, a medicine approved across Xywav's indications and once the company's best-selling medicine.

Xyrem started facing generic competition in the U.S. in 2023, and it no longer contributes much to Jazz's results. Other key growth drivers for the biotech company include Epidiolex, a medicine approved to treat seizures associated with certain rare forms of epilepsy. Epidiolex's second-quarter revenue grew 16% year over year to $292.1 million. Epidiolex appears to have a solid medium-term outlook. It likely won't face generic competition within the next five years, and Jazz Pharmaceuticals is seeking to launch it in other countries, including Japan.

But Xywav's prospects are a bit more uncertain, as the company faces competition from Lumryz, a medicine approved for cataplexy in narcolepsy patients with a more convenient once-nightly dosing schedule (Xywav is taken up to twice nightly). Xywav also continues to compete with generics for Xyrem. The drug has performed fairly well despite that, largely because of its lower sodium content (high sodium levels can cause serious health complications).

Still, given that this medicine accounted for about 39% of Jazz Pharmaceuticals' top line in the second quarter, competitive threats could weigh on the stock until the company successfully diversifies its portfolio. That's what it is doing thanks to its oncology lineup. In the second quarter, Jazz Pharmaceuticals' oncology revenue increased by almost 32% year over year to $362.3 million.

The company's Zepzelca, a lung cancer medicine, was the best-selling product in the oncology business. It posted sales of $105.8 million, up 42% year over year. The question is whether Jazz Pharmaceuticals' oncology segment can continue performing well over the medium term.

Jazz Pharmaceuticals is running more oncology clinical trials than in any other area, and one of its more promising products is called Ziihera. Jazz Pharmaceuticals first earned approval for this medicine in the U.S. in 2024. In August, this therapy earned a label expansion to treat certain patients with gastroesophageal adenocarcinoma. Ziihera is also being tested across at least half a dozen other potential indications.

Ziihera did not contribute much to sales in the second quarter, which isn't that surprising, given it hasn't been on the market for very long. But with its recent label expansion and potential additional indications, it could become a meaningful growth driver for the company. So could some of Jazz Pharmaceuticals' other candidates. The company earned U.S. approval for Modeyso, a medicine for diffuse midline glioma, last year, and it is also undergoing other clinical trials that could support label expansions.

Jazz's recent oncology launches look promising, and the company's deep pipeline offers significant opportunities to expand its market presence and boost sales growth, potentially allowing it to diversify away from Xywav. By 2031, the company could have a much stronger approved lineup that generates consistent sales and earnings growth. Does that make Jazz Pharmaceuticals a buy right now? My view is that the stock is attractive at current levels for investors willing to hold onto its shares for at least five years. In addition to Epidiolex, which could help drive sales growth for a while, and its improving oncology lineup, Jazz is trading at just 10.9x forward earnings, which compares very favorably with the 19.2x average for healthcare stocks.

#3420. Did Starbucks Just Give Investors Nearly 2.9 Billion Reasons to Buy Its Stock?

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Starbucks (SBUX +0.34%) is infusing its stock with a tiny shot of something good. Earlier this month, the unavoidable coffee giant declared its latest dividend raise, and the timing felt opportune -- the investor morale-boosting move came several weeks in advance of the company's fiscal fourth-quarter earnings release, scheduled for Wednesday after market close.

The hike wasn't particularly robust; nevertheless, it showed the company is concerned about how dividend investors perceive it. Let's dig into the latest payout improvement and see whether that, along with the company's recent performance and future potential, makes the stock a buy.

Starbucks announced that dividend raise on Oct. 7, stating that its board of directors approved a $0.01 increase in the quarterly common stock payout to $0.63 per share. This puts the annual amount at $2.52 per share, yielding just shy of 2.5%.

This keeps Starbucks' increasingly lengthy annual dividend raise streak alive, as the company has lifted the payout every year since initiating it in 2010.

And while a $0.01-per-share increase won't make income investors pounce on the stock, it'll enhance management's reputation for keeping shareholders happy. After all, with the move, Starbucks' annual dividend spend on its outstanding shares will reach nearly $2.9 billion.

Besides, Starbucks prefers to allocate capital for other purposes. In its dividend raise announcement, the company quoted CFO Cathy Smith as saying the raise "reflects our continued confidence and belief that disciplined execution of our Back to Starbucks strategy will drive sustainable long-term growth and value creation."

Despite the hopeful words, Starbucks saw a top-line slump in its fiscal third quarter. There's a big asterisk next to that fact, however, as the company divested a majority stake in its China business by forming a 60/40 licensed joint venture led by the domestic investment management firm Boyu Capital.

That means overall revenue from the sprawling Asian nation has declined, but what it earns today boosts the company's profit margins.

The China shift was a key reason why Starbucks posted that overall revenue decrease (of slightly over 1% year over year to $9.3 billion). A more revealing fundamental was global comparable store sales, which raced nearly 8% higher, crushing the 6.2% from a mere one quarter earlier.

Breaking that down further, North America comps were 8.1%, and international comps were 5.7%. With Starbucks no longer weighed down by operating the China cafes directly, that percentage surged -- it was more than double the second-quarter international figure of 2.6%.

Profitability leaped in the quarter; however, the headline net income number was skewed by that sale of the China stake. To a lesser extent, federal tariff refunds also added to the number, which, under generally accepted accounting principles (GAAP), surged 87% to almost $1.05 billion. Yet a more revealing line item, operating income, improved by 5% to $980 million.

Starbucks likes to attribute such improvements to the aforementioned Back to Starbucks -- the strategy captained by its star CEO, ex-Chipotle Mexican Grill chief Brian Niccol. He and his team hatched the initiative after he joined the company in 2024; it focuses on making the cafes more welcoming and tightening order fulfillment and inventory management, among other adjustments.

While I tend to take grand-sounding corporate initiatives with a grain of salt, I feel that there's some truth in the company's assertion.

I go to Starbucks cafes semi-regularly, and I've noticed more than a few of these changes; I do get my java quicker, it seems, and there's less "counter congestion" of food/beverage items at the pickup table thanks to changes in the ordering system.

So all this, plus the satisfying continuation of the company's streak of dividend raises, makes Starbucks a buy, right?

I wouldn't go that far. Yes, those improvements are tangible and feel as if they might be long-lasting, if not permanent, and the fundamentals are growing impressively of late. Yet if we look at the stock's valuations, they're high.

Forward P/E is over 32, making Starbucks' equity priced more like a growth stock, and I don't feel it's that dynamic a company, even with those recent (and solid) year-over-year improvements. I believe many investors are captivated by the turnaround story, but this is a company regaining its former strength and power, not one poised to vault ahead for years to come.

Yes, it's always pleasant when one of our companies raises a dividend. For me, though, Starbucks' latest hike isn't dramatic enough to buy the stock. I'd say the same for its recent upward lift in the fundamentals, as impressive as it has been.

#244. Israel election live: polls closing soon after historic turnout in vote set to decide Netanyahu’s future

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Israel awaits results after prime minister’s bid to extend voting hours failed

What you need to know about Israel’s election

It’s now past 10pm in Israel, which means polling stations across the country have closed after Israelis turned out in record numbers on a high-stakes election day that will determine the future of Benjamin Netanyahu.

A reminder that Israelis waiting in line at polling stations will still be allowed to cast their vote.

Israeli broadcasters are now set to air a set of exit polls, which will provide first estimates of the results. We’ll bring you all the latest on that as those come in.

Polling stations across Israel are set to close in less than 35 minutes, when news tv stations will release their first exit polls giving preliminary estimates of how many seats each party has won.

The first lot of official results then won’t be released until after around 1am local time on Wednesday. And while the exit polls usually provide a reliable indication of how people have voted, they can also vary.

The current coalition is expected to lose its majority, but we won’t expect clear results quickly as the coalition nature of Israeli government means that even small changes in vote share will change coalition calculations. We therefore may not get fuller results until the next day or the day after.

The final tally is expected by 30 October and official election results are formally published a week after the election, on 4 November.

After ​the final tally is ​in, Israel’s president, Isaac Herzog, ⁠will consult party leaders on whom they want as prime minister and then tap the candidate he believes has the best chance ​of forming a government.

That nominee has up to 42 days to form ​a government.

No ⁠single party in Israel has ever won an outright majority, so governments are formed by a coalition of parties who can get to 61 seats – and negotiations on forming a government often take weeks. Every Knesset seat counts and ⁠small ​parties can have a big impact by becoming kingmakers.

If ​the nominee fails, others can get a chance to try. If no government emerges, another election is called, meaning ​Benjamin Netanyahu and his cabinet would remain in place as a “caretaker” government.

It’s now just gone 9pm in Israel, and nationwide voter turnout as of 8pm was 74.3%, per the Central Elections Committee.

That is not only up from 66.3% at the same time in the 2022 election; that figure surpasses the 70.6% total recorded turnout on election day in 2022.

The governor of New York, home to the world’s largest urban Jewish community outside of Israel, has said that her job would get “a lot easier” if Benjamin Netanyahu loses this election.

In an interview with Politico, Kathy Hochul said:

double quotation markI represent the largest [urban] Jewish population in the world outside of Israel. And I know they’re suffering. A lot of people are anxious about antisemitism, which is sky-high right now …

I went to Israel right after October 7. I saw a lot of pain there. But I actually cautioned the government there, in private conversations, not to overreach.

The world is with you when it comes to the brutal assault by … Hamas on innocent Israeli people. An overreach could change that dynamic, and that’s what’s happened.

We have to have leadership there that understands the security of Israel is paramount - critically important to me, but also the world - but to have a safe Palestinian state as well, which has been talked about my entire life.

I still believe that this can happen. I believe we have to get people of goodwill who can make this happen, and as long as Netanyahu is sitting there, that is not in his interest to do that. It’s just not, and it won’t happen.

Voter turnout as of 6pm local time reached 66.7%, the highest ever by that hour since voting hours were first recorded, the Central Elections Committee said.

That is a 9% increase from the 2022 election at that time and Israel’s highest recorded turnout by 6pm.

The Central Elections Committee decided not to extend voting hours beyond 10pm, rejecting a request submitted by Netanyahu bloc parties, Haaretz is reporting.

In a statement, the committee said it met this evening and decided “the current circumstances do not justify an extension”.

It added that the high turnout “reflects strong public engagement in the democratic process” and “no need to extend voting hours was identified”.

A reminder that, under Israeli law, the current rules do allow for a voter to cast their vote past the polling time, just as long they arrive at a station by 10pm.

As we’ve been reporting, a record number of Israelis cast their ballots in the first half of polling day, voting at polling stations across the country and in the Israeli-occupied West Bank.

By 2pm local time (it is now after 8pm), 44.9% of voters had cast their ballot – the highest turnout since record-keeping began in the 1970s.

It’s been described as an existential moment; a battle for the soul of a nation. With voting under way in Israel today, public opinion is divided on what the future of the country looks like. To his supporters, Benjamin Netanyahu has protected the country in a way no other leader could, taking on Iran, Hamas, Hezbollah and Houthis.

To his critics, Netanyahu is to blame for a catastrophic failure of security, leaving Israel more isolated, traumatised and no safer. Today, his most serious challenge comes from a candidate with little experience in office. Gadi Eisenkot is seen as humble, approachable … and honest. The former chief of staff for the Israeli military is promising a future without corruption. Thirteen months ago, his party didn’t even exist.

But after three years of war and upheaval, it’s clear that the consequences of today’s elections go way beyond Israel’s borders.

In the latest edition of our Today in Focus podcast, Emma Graham-Harrison tells Nosheen Iqbal about the stakes of the election and what it could all mean for the future of Israel – and for the Palestinians living under occupation.

In the Palestinian Christian town of Taybeh, Ahmed Tibi, the leader of another Joint List party, Ta’al, had a shouting match with one of the Israeli right wing party representatives over delays, complaining that a woman had fainted while waiting in line.

At a polling station in a Nazareth school this afternoon, an election worker, Bilal Sha’awan shrugged off the far right’s tactics as “childsplay”.

At 3pm there were no queues and a steady flow of voters. A representative of Itamar Ben-Gvir’s Jewish Power party, a young man dressed in the style of settler “hilltop youth”, dressed casually, with a knitted kippa and long sidelocks, strode past Sha’awan, but gave no comment.

Manar Hassan, an observer from Ra’am, the other significant Palestinian Israeli party, described the turnout as “amazing”.

“It’s never been this strong,” she said, adding that the reasons for the higher level participation were not hard to find.

“We witnessed a catastrophe in Gaza,” Hassan said. “Here we live in fear and anxiety. We need drastic change.”

Another hourly voter turn-out update. As has been well established by now this is the largest turnout on record (since the early 1970s when such things were first measured).

As of 6pm local time, 66.7% of eligible voters had cast a ballot. That’s a 9% increase since the last election in 2022.

We can also expect a surge in the Palestinian Israeli vote – which accounts for about 20% of the population – as they tend to vote after work. We should hear shortly the decision of the Central Electoral Committee on whether they will grant the Netanyahu bloc’s request to extend voting hours past 10pm local time. At the moment, polls are due to close in about 2.5 hours.

Tens of thousands of Israelis who live overseas have flown back to Israel to vote. Around 800,000 eligible Israeli voters live outside the country, over 10 percent of the potential electorate, according to Ha’aretz newspaper, but only diplomats can cast a ballot overseas.

For everyone else, taking part in elections requires investing considerable amounts of time and money to fly back.

Yuval Schwarzfuchs, 26, travelled from Rio de Janeiro via Ethiopia, to vote for the Palestinian-led Joint List. “For me, its about equality for all,” he said. “I did what I had to do.”

He came to the Jerusalem polling station wearing a T-shirt with an image of Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, the left-wing President of Brazil, who defied polls to win re-election at the weekend.

That victory gave some hope to opponents of Israel’s right-wing government.

“Netanyahu does have a good campaign but I think he will loose,” said Schwarzfuchs, who was at the polling station with his brother, who also voted for the Joint List.

Up to 67,000 voters may be returning to Israel, according to AID Coalition, a U.S.-based nonprofit focused on strengthening Israel’s democracy, so this constituency could be key in a tight race.

In Israel’s proportional representation system, a few thousand votes can have a significant impact on results and around 40,000 votes is expected to secure a seat in the Knesset.

AID coalition organised a “Fly&Vote” initiative to support Israelis who wanted to return for this election, and their figure is based on surveys and registration data. About half of returnees are flying in from the United States, with others from Europe, Canada, Australia and New Zealand.

The project is bipartisan, and not everyone returning to vote opposes the current government, but anecdotal evidence suggests many are opposition supporters who share the view that Israel faces an existential choice.

Monica Moria, 58, and her husband Ron Moria flew in to Tel Aviv from Barcelona. Then they took a train to the kibbutz Kabri in northern Israel, where she is registered to vote, where Moria cast her ballot for the most left-wing of the Jewish Israeli parties, the Democrats.

There were at least five other people at the kibbutz who had flown in to vote, including one who came from Australia and another from the United States.

double quotation markIt feels like Israel has lost its way, there are deep divisions and hatreds that will be very difficult to heal. The idea is to bring a political change so that we can bring Israeli society together again. I want to be optimistic, but I am also afraid.

Kobi Mizrahi, 35, who’s been completing a PhD in New York said he had paid more than $1,600 to come back to Israel for just a few days.

double quotation markGiven the fact that it feels like a “now or never” moment, where if we lose these elections to the Bibi coalition, this might be the turning point we can’t come back to. I felt like I have to do anything in my power to help.

He said the Netanyahu government had abandoned their basic responsibilities, in security, education, transport. “The government isn’t working for us [but] only to maintain political power”.

double quotation markUnfortunately in the past years things have become worse and worse, and in particular after October 7th, when we needed the government the most. Continuing in this direction may bring us, in every aspect, to a point of no return. This is true politically (with extreme parties gaining more power), economically, and morally. The goals of politicians like Betzalel Smotrich to expand settlements in the West Bank hurt all other Israelis in every aspect of our life.

Amid the record turnout, other parties in the Netanyahu coalition camp have joined his Likud party’s request for voting hours to be extended. At the moment, polls are due to close in just under three hours, by 10pm local time. Shas, led by Aryeh Deri, and Amcha Yisrael, led by Ofer Winter, have requested one-hour extensions until 11pm.

Shas said it had requested the extension to “prevent a situation in which citizens waiting in line would be unable to vote.”

However the current rules do allow for a voter to cast their vote past the polling time, just as long they arrive at a station by 10pm.

Most polls show Netanyahu’s Likud and his current coalition parters losing their majority. That fall in voter support has been fairly consistent for more than two years.

They also show a clear anti-Netanyahu majority.

However, those opposition seats are divided between the bloc of Jewish Israeli parties, led by Eisenkot’s Yashar, and parties representing Palestinian citizens of Israel.

The Jewish Israeli parties are unlikely to have a majority on their own, but have mostly ruled out going into coalition with Palestinians.

Palestinian politicians have indicated they might support a minority government of the Jewish Israeli parties in order to unseat Netanyahu’s far-right coalition.

While Jewish Israeli and Palestinian Israeli parties teaming up to form a coalition is seen as a bit taboo – particularly after anti-Arab sentiment rose in the wake of the October 7 attacks – it is not unprecedented. The Ra’am leader, Mansour Abbas, in 2021 took his party into a historic coalition government with Naftali Bennett, now part of the opposition bloc, and Netanyahu himself had previously started coalition negotiations with Abbas. Polling from Channel 12 on the weekend showed too that among the ‘Change’ bloc – what Eisenkot, Bennett, Galan and Liberman are calling their alliance – 60% of voters would prefer a minority government formed with the support of Ra’am from the outside, compared to 20% who would favour repeat elections, and 10% who would want the opposition to unite with Netanyahu.

Here’s a round-up of the some of the party leaders and politicians casting their votes today. If I’m missing a few that’s because I haven’t seen pictures of them voting yet. Netanyahu bloc

His Likud party said in the past hour they’d requested an extension to voting hours from electoral authorities.

He and far-right allies have been running a racist scare campaign today, urging their base to show up to a vote which they claimed was being “stolen”.

The main opposition party leading in the polls, Yashar, has come out saying it “firmly opposes” any extension to voting hours. Polls are open for roughly another 3.5 hours, until 10pm local time.

Gadi Eisenkot’s party called it an attempt to “lay the groundwork for allegations challenging the integrity of the election”.

The Central Election Committee (CEC) – the country’s electoral authorities – is meeting in an hour to consider the request. There have also been several complaints filed by political parties in the anti-Netanyahu bloc to the committee today.

Yashar said it had contacted the CEC following reports some of its ballot slips had been removed or damaged at polling stations

Naftali Bennett’s Together ( B’Yachad) party said it had received reports that an app was being used to monitor voters at polling stations in northern Israel

The Civil Election Guard, a volunteer force of election monitors, said ballot slips for the Haredi Public party in ultra-Orthodox communities had been removed from voting booths or hidden underneath other parties’ papers, according to Haaretz reporting.

Israelis are turning out in record high numbers to vote in an election that many describe as critical. The urgency among opposition voters is even higher than supporters of Netanyahu and his coalition, analysts say.

One of the voters who considers this an existential moment for Israel is Nir Honi, who changed his name by deed poll and has spent eight days inside a small stone circle outside the president’s residence, dressed in biblical- style clothing, in a one-man call for change.

His protest harks back to the story of a first-century Jewish scholar named Honi who convinced God to end a drought with a similar protest.

“(Honi) drew a circle around himself with a stick, and said ‘dear God, I will not move from here until you have mercy on your children and bring us rain’,” the newly renamed Nir Honi said. “My problem is not rain, its a more spiritual issue.”

“We have been in a continuous disaster since October 7th 2023,” he said.

double quotation markWe did not learn anything, we did not investigate, the same government continued and they want to continue for four more years.”

So eight days ago the long-term activist changed his last name to Nir Honi and launched his protest. He has left only once, to vote.

double quotation markOn Monday, which is called the day of the prayer for rain, I came here, I drew a circle in front of the residence of the president of Israel and I said, ‘Dear God, I will not move from here until the October 7th government moves’. From that moment on I have lived in this small circle.”

The Palestinian Israeli vote is a critical camp in this election, representing about one fifth of all eligible voters.

Their votes are expected to go to either Ra’am, led by Mansour Abbas, or Joint List, an electoral alliance of Palestinian-Israeli parties.

But for them them, change will not be straightforward, as my colleague Julian Borger in Nazareth writes.

Even if the opposition bloc led by Gadi Eisenkot wins more seats than Netanyahu’s coalition, it is not expected to win an outright majority on its own, and it has so far ruled out going into coalition with the Palestinian Israeli parties, who could well hold the balance of power ones the votes are counted.

Amjad Shbita, the general secretary of Hadash, the leading party in the Joint List coalition, thought that the Eisenkot’s approach might change after the elections.

“He cannot sit down [in coalition] with Netanyahu, and he won’t want to go back to another round of elections, so we think he can be flexible,” he said.

In Nazareth, the largest Palestinian-majority town in Israel, the turnout at polling stations has been noticeably stronger than last elections. By 2pm the turnout was over 32%, up from 28% at this time four years ago. That was still below the average Jewish turnout across the country, but a surge of evening voting is expected here as voters come home from work. At the headquarters of the Joint List, the biggest coalition of Palestinian-Israeli parties, the most pressing concern was the spoiler tactics of far-right election monitors at the predominantly Palestinian polling stations. “They can say that they want to check all the voter numbers and identity and everything, so they can slow things down, so that people wait a long time and give up. That’s their intention,” Amjad Shbita, the general secretary of Hadash, a socialist movement which is the leading party in the Joint List coalition. At the worst, these tactical ploys have slowed the process down so that it took 30 minutes to vote, rather than just a couple, he said. The Joint List sent its workers out to the polling station to provide water and folding chairs so that voters would not lose patience and go home. Shbita said that Palestinian Israel motivation is strong enough to overcome such obstacles. “It is the first time for a long time that a government has stayed in office for its full term, four years,” he said. “We are talking about one of the worst governments when it comes to Arab society. We feel racism at all levels of Israeli society, in hospitals, in buses, in public transportation, in the media. So people understand that today we have the opportunity to change the government.”

#1620. Opinion - That's that: Liverpool gifted sensational midfielder for €70m

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Liverpool look like they've got a free run to sign one of Europe's top midfielders. Can anyone even stop them from here?

Liverpool need a midfielder. We’ve said that for a while, dating back into last season as their control over the middle of the pitch was pretty weak for a team hoping to compete at the very top of the game.

The Reds lacked - and still lack - either someone to consistently win the ball back or someone to dictate the middle of the pitch. We thought they’d move in the summer but instead sold Curtis Jones, arguably their best at the latter, and didn’t replace him.

The start of the season has shown a real dependence on Trey Nyoni and while he’s clearly a huge talent, he’s also a teenager. We’re not sure Liverpool will want to be relying on him come the business end of the season, honestly, as we do think they’ll have a look at the market in January.

However, they won’t be signing Alex Scott. Ben Jacobs has outlined that Chelsea are in a very, very strong position to sign the player from Bournemouth, even securing a verbal agreement.

That does have a counter-effect, though. And it’s one that should see Liverpool land a different signing.

Lamine Camara looks destined for LiverpoolLamine Camara has been heavily linked with Liverpool for a while now. Ever since the last few weeks of the summer transfer window, really. Camara was ‘supposed’ to join Chelsea but AS Monaco pulled out of a deal at the deadline as they felt they could secure a bigger fee in 2027. Liverpool reportedly alerted them that they’d pay the asking price next year. Their rivals remained Chelsea but it now appears at the Blues are out of the running.

Lamine Camara has been heavily linked with Liverpool for a while now. Ever since the last few weeks of the summer transfer window, really. Camara was ‘supposed’ to join Chelsea but AS Monaco pulled out of a deal at the deadline as they felt they could secure a bigger fee in 2027.

Liverpool reportedly alerted them that they’d pay the asking price next year. Their rivals remained Chelsea but it now appears at the Blues are out of the running.

That essentially leaves Liverpool as far and away the leaders for Camara and he’s fully expected to move somewhere next year. It would now be a massive surprise, quite honestly, if that wasn’t Anfield.

We’d be hugely in favour of that as Camara ticks many of the boxes Liverpool are after. The Senegal star has started the Ligue 1 season in magnificent form, too, and looks like a player who may have stepped up another level after having already shone brightly last year.

If Camara is indeed destined for Liverpool, and we imagine he is, then they’re getting a fantastic player. No question about that.

#4943. DEX Aggregators Explained: How They Work and How to Choose One

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Home » Cryptopedia » DEX Aggregators Explained: How They Work and How to Choose One

Discover how DEX aggregators find trading routes, compare liquidity, and help you make more informed crypto swaps.

Decentralized exchanges (DEXs) allow users to trade cryptocurrencies directly through blockchain-based protocols. However, prices, liquidity, and trading costs can vary across platforms, making it difficult to identify the most efficient way to execute a swap.

A DEX aggregator addresses this problem by searching multiple liquidity sources and helping you to find suitable trading routes.

Understanding how DEX aggregators work is useful for anyone trading decentralized finance (DeFi) assets. It also helps explain the broader role of crypto aggregators, the challenges of cross-chain liquidity, and the factors that matter when comparing available solutions.

A DEX aggregator is a service that searches multiple decentralized exchanges (DEX) or liquidity sources to identify potential routes for a crypto swap. Instead of requiring you to compare individual exchanges manually, it brings relevant trading options together through a single interface.

The main objective is to improve trade execution by considering factors such as the quoted exchange rate, available liquidity, price impact, and transaction costs.

For example, a user swapping one token for another might find that a single DEX does not offer the most favorable rate for the desired trade size. Another platform may have deeper liquidity, while a third route may offer a better overall result after accounting for fees.

A DEX aggregator evaluates the available options according to its routing logic and the information accessible to it.

DEX aggregators are one category of crypto aggregators. The broader term can also refer to services that collect cryptocurrency prices, market data, or information from multiple platforms. DEX aggregators specifically focus on decentralized trading and swap execution.

Although implementations differ, most DEX aggregators follow a similar process.

1. Search available liquidity sources. The aggregator checks supported decentralized exchanges, liquidity pools, or other eligible sources for routes that can facilitate the requested swap.

2. Evaluate possible trading routes. It compares available options based on factors such as expected output, liquidity, price impact, and applicable costs. The exact criteria depend on the aggregator’s routing algorithm.

3. Select or construct a route. A swap may use one liquidity source or involve multiple pools. Some aggregators can split a trade across several routes when doing so is expected to improve the result.

4. Prepare the transaction. The selected route is translated into the transactions or smart contract calls needed to execute the swap. Depending on the protocol, the user may need to approve a token allowance before trading.

5. Execute the swap. The transaction is submitted to the relevant blockchain. The final result depends on execution conditions, including available liquidity, network congestion, and changes in token prices.

A quoted price is not necessarily the price at which a trade will settle. Conditions can change between quote generation and transaction execution, and a transaction may fail or revert.

The aggregator’s routing capabilities, supported protocols, and execution model therefore matter as much as the initial quote.

A decentralized exchange provides the infrastructure for users to trade tokens, typically through smart contracts and liquidity pools or other decentralized trading mechanisms.

A DEX aggregator connects to multiple trading venues or liquidity sources and searches for suitable ways to execute a swap.

An aggregator does not automatically guarantee a better price. Its result depends on which sources it supports, the accuracy of its quotes, its routing logic, and the costs involved in executing the transaction.

Liquidity fragmentation occurs when available trading liquidity is distributed across different exchanges, pools, blockchain networks, or trading pairs.

This matters because the amount of liquidity available at a particular price affects how much a user can trade without significantly moving the market price.

Consider a token swap with several possible liquidity sources. One pool may offer an attractive price for a small trade but insufficient depth for a larger one. Another pool may have more liquidity but a different exchange rate. The most suitable route depends on the trade size and the total cost of execution.

Three concepts help explain these differences:

These factors are related but not interchangeable. A route with a favorable quoted exchange rate may still be less attractive once price impact and transaction costs are considered.

DEX aggregators help address liquidity fragmentation within the networks and sources they support. However, they cannot eliminate insufficient liquidity or guarantee the best possible execution in every market condition.

A cross-chain DEX aggregator helps users find or execute swap routes involving assets on different blockchain networks. It extends the aggregation concept beyond liquidity sources available within a single network.

This introduces additional complexity. Blockchains maintain separate states, use different transaction mechanisms, and may have different liquidity providers and security assumptions.

Depending on the solution, a cross-chain swap may involve a cross-chain bridge, a cross-chain messaging protocol, liquidity networks, or other mechanisms that facilitate transfers and transactions between networks.

A cross-chain route can therefore involve more than finding a favorable token exchange rate. It may also require accounting for transfer costs, execution time, supported networks, and the risks associated with the underlying infrastructure.

When evaluating cross-chain aggregators, it is important to distinguish between two capabilities:

The most suitable DEX aggregator depends on the assets being traded, the networks involved, and the user’s priorities. Comparing a few practical criteria can help identify the relevant differences.

No single criterion determines which aggregator is best for every trade. Comparing the expected final output, transaction costs, supported routes, and relevant security assumptions provides a more useful basis for evaluation than relying on a headline claim or advertised feature list.

DEX aggregators simplify route discovery, but they do not remove the risks of decentralized trading.

Users should review transaction details, understand the applicable risks, and verify token addresses and network information before confirming a swap.

DEX aggregators make decentralized trading easier to navigate by bringing multiple liquidity sources and potential swap routes into one interface. Their value depends on how effectively they evaluate available liquidity, account for transaction costs, and execute trades.

When comparing crypto aggregators, the most useful approach is to look beyond quoted prices. Supported networks, expected output, routing transparency, execution reliability, and security all contribute to the overall trading experience.

As DeFi expands across different protocols and blockchains, knowing how DEX aggregators work can help you choose the right solution for your trading needs.

A DEX aggregator searches supported decentralized exchanges and liquidity sources to identify potential trading routes. Its goal is to help users compare or optimize swap execution without manually checking every venue.

They serve different purposes. A DEX provides trading infrastructure, while an aggregator searches across supported sources. An aggregator may find a more favorable route, but the result depends on liquidity, fees, routing quality, and execution conditions.

Not necessarily. Some services may not charge a separate aggregation fee, but swaps can still involve blockchain gas fees, protocol fees, or other costs. The applicable charges depend on the service, network, and route.

A DEX aggregator can search liquidity sources across decentralized exchanges, often within one blockchain network. A cross-chain aggregator coordinates routes involving different blockchain networks, which can introduce additional transfer mechanisms, costs, and security considerations.

Their safety depends on the contracts, protocols, and infrastructure involved. Aggregation does not eliminate smart contract vulnerabilities, market risks, or cross-chain security concerns. Users should assess the specific service and transaction before proceeding.

#5354. Waymo adds nearly 250 square miles of robotaxi coverage in 5 cities

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Waymo is growing its driverless service area by nearly 250 square miles over the next week, spread across Los Angeles, Nashville, Orlando, San Diego, and the San Francisco Bay Area.

That’s about as big as Tesla’s entire Houston Robotaxi geofence after this week’s expansion, and Waymo is adding it in cities where it already runs with nobody in the driver’s seat.

The company announced the expansion in a short post on its blog today. Here’s what changes in each market, with Waymo’s updated service area maps:

Service now runs down the coast into the South Bay, through Manhattan Beach and Redondo Beach to Torrance.

The zone pushes further south, and riders can now get to the zoo.

Coverage on the east side nearly doubles, and there’s more service downtown.

The service area now stretches north to La Jolla, taking in University City and Clairemont along the way.

Waymo is pushing further into the South Bay, including a connection to BART at Berryessa station in San Jose. The service area now runs from San Francisco down the Peninsula to San Jose and Campbell.

Waymo didn’t break out square mileage by city, and it didn’t say how many vehicles it’s adding to cover the new ground. The updated maps are already in the Waymo app.

Some of these markets are very young. Waymo opened Nashville to public riders in April with a zone of about 60 square miles covering downtown, Midtown, Music Row, and East Nashville. San Diego only went public on September 1, with roughly 50 square miles between the beach and I-15 that barely crossed north of I-8. So getting to La Jolla less than two months later is a big jump.

In May, Waymo pushed its coverage past 1,400 square miles across 11 cities, which is bigger than the state of Rhode Island. Since then, it went driverless in Las Vegas, launched San Diego, and now says it operates in 15 US metros with more than 500,000 paid rides per week. It’s even mapping Toronto for a 2028 launch.

Tesla is also drawing bigger maps. After launching Robotaxi in Dallas and Houston with tiny geofences in April, it stretched Houston to about 254 square miles this week, according to Robotaxi Tracker. But the tracker counts 34 vehicles in Houston.

And on the metric that reflects actual use, it’s not close. Waymo logged 3.9 million rider-only miles per week in Q2 2026, while Tesla’s driverless fleet did 558,000 miles in its latest reported week, mostly in Austin. That’s about 7x, and it comes on the same day Elon Musk claimed the robotaxi race against Waymo “was never a real contest.”

I like this. Good progress. But I’ll be truly impressed when the Bay Area service area extends south into LA, expands north, and the same happens in San Diego.

It’s going to happen. Waymo is already dabbling with highway operations in California.I can see them linking all those service areas within the next 2 years. Maybe even by the end of next year if all goes well.

#3176. Neil Young sues Trump committee for $10M for playing 'Rockin' in the Free World' at rally

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Legendary musician Neil Young filed a $10 million lawsuit against President Donald Trump's campaign committee for infringing Young's copyright by playing his song "Rockin' in the Free World" just before the president took the stage to speak at an Oct. 12  rally in Youngstown, Ohio.

The Trump 47 Committee has caused that song, without the musician's permission, "to be played numerous times at other rallies designed to encourage Republican votes in the upcoming mid-term elections," Young's lawsuit in U.S. District Court in Richmond, Virginia, alleges.

Trump "was personally aware that a license would be required to play the Song at rallies," and that Young "has not, and would not, grant the required license," the suit filed Monday says.

Young, 80, in 2020 sued another corporation formed by Trump, Donald J. Trump for President Inc., in Manhattan federal court for copyright infringement of "Rockin' in the Free World," and another song written by Young, "Devil's Sidewalk," for having played the songs without permission at campaign rallies.

In that first case, Trump's corporation "asserted no defense and immediately settled shortly after it was served, promising that it would never again use 'Rockin' in the Free World' without a license and permission from Young," the new suit said.

CNBC has requested comment from the Trump 47 Committee about the suit, and from Young's lawyers.

The White House, when asked for comment, referred CNBC to the Republican National Committee, which did not immediately respond to an email seeking comment.

Young, who was born in Canada, is a naturalized U.S. citizen. He is a two-time inductee in the Rock & Roll Hall of Fame: in 1995 as a solo artist, and in 1997 as a member of Buffalo Springfield. Young was also a member of the folk rock supergroup Crosby, Stills, Nash & Young.

Young released "Rockin' in the Free World" in 1989.

His lawsuit describes the tune as a "blistering anthem that contrasts the hollow promises of American political rhetoric with the harsh realities faced by the poor, homeless and forgotten."

The lyrics include descriptions of a mother discarding her baby, addiction, and "garbage piling in the shadows of prosperity — while the repeated chorus delivers savage irony, celebrating freedom that clearly isn't shared by all," the suit notes.

"In short, the Song is the opposite of everything that Donald J. Trump stands for," the suit says.

In addition to the Trump 47 Committee, the suit lists five other defendants, identified by the pseudonym "Doe," whom Young believes are liable for infringing on Young's copyright.

The Oct. 12 Trump rally that is referenced in the lawsuit was also the scene of an incident that has drawn attention from local law enforcement. Videos from the rally show several attendees apparently attacking and beating a woman, Blake Miley, who had repeatedly yelled 'fascist" at Trump while wearing a shirt bearing the message "Release the Epstein Files."

Mahoning County, Ohio, Sheriff Jerry Greene reportedly is investigating three individuals in connection with the incident.

#253. Legacy of 7 October looms large as a divided Israel goes to the polls in record numbers

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Bereaved families tell voters to remember the victims while others have flown thousands of miles to cast their ballots

Election day in Israel began with a protest by some of the voters with most at stake. Bereaved families of those killed in the 7 October attacks held a minute of silence at 6.29 am, the moment when Hamas launched its onslaught just over three years ago.

They gathered in a Tel Aviv plaza that has become known as “hostages square” because it was the centre of demonstrations demanding the return of more than 200 captives from Gaza. Holding pictures of their loved ones, they stood in silence then called on voters to remember the losses and betrayals of that day when deciding on the next government.

“Three years after the massacre, our loved ones will not be voting today. Go out and vote – remember them at the ballot box. This is what these elections are about,” the group said.

They are not explicitly backing any political party, but one of their key demands is a state commission of inquiry into the attacks. Benjamin Netanyahu has resisted this and it has become a rallying point for the opposition parties trying to unseat him as prime minister.

Netanyahu’s strongest challenger, Gadi Eisenkot, started the day by visiting communities attacked on 7 October. “If someone in the government had a shred of responsibility, they would start the morning here,” said the former military chief and leader of the Yashar party.

Standing in a cheering crowd, Eisenkot called on all Israelis to vote to get rid of “the worst government Israel has known”. He noted that victory wasn’t certain, adding that “we are on perhaps the most critical day since the founding of the state”.

As the day wore on, it became clear that voter turnout was shattering all records and among those lining up at polling stations were tens of thousands of Israelis who live overseas.

A bipartisan “Fly&Vote” initiative was set up to support those who wanted to return because electoral law only allows voting in person. While not everyone coming back was opposed to the government, anecdotal evidence suggested many were opposition supporters who held the view that Israel faced an existential choice.

Monica Moria, 58, and her husband, Ron, flew in to Tel Aviv from Barcelona. They took a train to a kibbutz where she is registered to vote and she cast her ballot for the most leftwing of the Jewish Israeli parties, the Democrats.

She said: “It feels like Israel has lost its way, there are deep divisions and hatreds that will be very difficult to heal. The idea is to bring a political change so that we can bring Israeli society together again. I want to be optimistic, but I am also afraid.”

Kobi Mizrahi, 35, who has been completing a PhD in New York, paid more than $1,600 (£1,200) to come back to Israel for just a few days and vote.

He said: “Given the fact that it feels like a ‘now or never’ moment, where if we lose these elections to the Bibi coalition, this might be the turning point we can’t come back to. I felt like I have to do anything in my power to help.”

Yuval Schwarzfuchs, 26, travelled from Rio de Janeiro via Ethiopia to vote for the Palestinian-led Joint List. “For me, its about equality for all,” he said. “I did what I had to do.”

He came to the Jerusalem polling station wearing a T-shirt with an image of Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, the leftwing president of Brazil, who defied the polls to win re-election at the weekend.

In the settlement of Efrat near Jerusalem, Shani, 19, had come to the polling station with her parents, to cast her vote for the far-right party of the finance minister, Bezalel Smotrich.

She is part of a large cohort of first-time voters who are more rightwing than older generations and may help bolster support for Netanyahu and his far-right coalition partners.

More than 500,000 Israelis have reached voting age since the last election in 2022, and three-quarters of first-time Jewish voters define themselves as right or centre-right, a poll for Channel 12 television found (it did not survey Palestinian citizens of Israel).

Shani said she chose Smotrich, a settler himself, for his promises to expand Israel’s footprint in occupied Palestine. “Smotrich is pushing forward with settlement expansion and improving security,” she said.

Her mother, kindergarten teacher Rivka, also voted for Smotrich, and said her priority was to return Netanyahu’s coalition to office.

“The right must win, because otherwise there is a chance of a Palestinian state, which will be very bad for Jews,” she said.

Spiralling attacks by militant Israeli settlers on Palestinians, and the growing threat of sanctions on settlement goods and construction, were not a key election issue for voters in Efrat who spoke to the Guardian.

The Palestinian Israeli vote is a critical camp in this election, representing about a fifth of eligible voters. Their votes are expected to go to either Ra’am, led by Mansour Abbas, or Joint List, an electoral alliance of Palestinian-Israeli parties.

Even if the opposition bloc led by Eisenkot wins more seats than Netanyahu’s coalition, it is not expected to win an outright majority. It has also ruled out going into coalition with the Palestinian Israeli parties, which could well hold the balance of power.

Amjad Shbita, the general secretary of Hadash, the leading party in the Joint List coalition, thinks Eisenkot’s approach may change after the elections.

“He cannot sit down [in coalition] with Netanyahu, and he won’t want to go back to another round of elections, so we think he can be flexible,” he said.

In Nazareth, the largest Palestinian-majority town in Israel, the turnout at polling stations has been noticeably higher than at recent elections, although still below the average Jewish turnout across the country. A surge of evening voting was expected here as people came home from work.

At the headquarters of the Joint List, the most pressing concern was the spoiler tactics of far-right election monitors at predominantly Palestinian polling stations.

“They can say that they want to check all the voter numbers and identity and everything, so they can slow things down, so that people wait a long time and give up. That’s their intention,” said Shbita.

At worst, these ploys have slowed the process down so that it took half an hour to vote, rather than a couple of minutes, he added.

Shbita said voters were sufficiently motivated to overcome such obstacles. “It is the first time for a long time that a government has stayed in office for its full term, four years,” he said.

“We are talking about one of the worst governments when it comes to Arab society. We feel racism at all levels of Israeli society, in hospitals, in buses, in public transportation, in the media. So people understand that today we have the opportunity to change the government.”

#3445. PayPal Just Raised Its Profit Forecast, and the Stock Barely Moved. Here’s Why

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

At first glance, PayPal's (PYPL +2.00%) third-quarter results looked rather strong. Adjusted EPS grew by 8% year-over-year and handily beat analyst estimates, and management raised its full-year profit guidance.

However, the stock initially fell modestly after the earnings report, and as of 2 p.m. ET on earnings day, it was higher by just 1%, despite the impressive earnings beat and guidance raise. Here's what is worrying investors, and why a beat and raise isn't enough to move the stock higher.

In the third quarter, PayPal's revenue grew by just 2% to $8.58 billion, which fell short of expectations. Transaction margin dollars, a gross profit indicator, rose by only 2%.

The bigger issue is branded checkout. This is the PayPal button you can click on when shopping online. It's PayPal's most profitable business, but payment volume grew by just 1% on a currency-neutral basis. That is the fourth consecutive quarter that we've seen branded checkout growth in the 1% to 2% range. The company's transaction take rate, which is the share of each dollar it keeps as revenue, declined to 1.58% from 1.64% a year earlier, and management is only forecasting slight growth in transaction margin dollars for the fourth quarter.

To be fair, several parts of PayPal's business are performing quite well. Venmo is a standout, with payment volume growing 14% year over year. Buy now, pay later (BNPL) volume grew 25%. And payment volume on PayPal and Venmo debit cards in the United States grew by more than 60%.

These are all becoming significant growth engines for the business, and management expects financial services products like these to make up more than 20% of transaction margin dollars this year. But that still leaves 80% of transaction margin dollars coming from low-growth revenue streams.

PayPal grew its earnings per share by 8%, despite growing gross profit by just 2%. How did this happen? The answer is that cost reductions and buybacks are the primary drivers of earnings growth. The company has eliminated over 2,000 roles and is targeting over $1.5 billion in annual savings over the next few years, and PayPal has spent $6 billion buying back its own stock over the past 12 months, reducing its weighted share count by 11%.

Don't get me wrong. Cost-cutting and buybacks certainly can create value. But it's not the quality earnings growth we'd get from the core business growing again, which is what investors really want to see.

The bottom line is that PayPal trades for less than 10 times its expected 2026 earnings, so there isn't exactly a ton of growth priced in. The market wants branded checkout to grow again, but it isn't confident that management can deliver. And until it does, earnings beats that are primarily fueled by expense reductions and buybacks aren't likely to move the stock too much. I'm holding onto my shares, but I'll be watching branded checkout volume very closely over the next few quarters.

Matt Frankel, CFP® has positions in PayPal and has the following options: long January 2027 $75 calls on PayPal, long January 2027 $95 calls on PayPal, short January 2027 $135 calls on PayPal, and short January 2027 $85 calls on PayPal. #5394. Look Ma, No Inverter! New Ford Fathom Cuts The Cost Of Bidirectional EV Charging

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Ford Motor Company is still disguising the soon-to-arrive Fathom electric pickup truck under a layer of camouflage, but the iconic automaker is more than happy to share what’s going on inside. In the latest news drop, Ford wants everyone to know that you don’t need an expensive inverter to convert the truck into a mobile, zero emission generating station for your home.

The idea of using an EV battery to power household appliances seemed pretty futuristic back in the early 2010’s, but nowadays the technology is commonplace. The key is bidirectional charging technology, which enables an EV battery to discharge electricity as well as receive it from a charging station.

One obvious use is backup emergency power, where EV batteries have the advantage of quiet, pollution-free running — unlike gas or diesel generators. In case of a prolonged power outage, drivers can travel outside of the blackout area, charge up, and return with a  fully charged battery to keep the lights on at home.

EV batteries can also help ratepayers avoid higher peak demand electricity rates. Households with home EV charging stations can time their charging sessions to take advantage of utility discounts for off-peak use, and discharge their battery to avoid high rates during peak periods.

The sticky wicket, of course, is cost. Generally speaking, home EV charging stations are not free, and neither is the inverter needed to convert the DC current from an EV battery into an AC current for household use.

Ford’s answer is a built-in system that eliminates the need for an external inverter. “Our goal wasn’t just to add bidirectional power to our EVs. It was to redesign the system to cost far less so more people can access bidirectional power,” Ford Model e general manager Kay Hart explained in a new post earlier today.

“With Fathom offering bidirectional power at an MSRP starting below $30,000, even more people can benefit from potential energy savings and home backup power,” Hart added.

That seems to suggest that the cost is included in the company’s target MSRP of $28,350, though in another part of the post Hart uses the word “available,” which typically means an add-on:

“In 2027, the all-new Ford Fathom™ electric truck will start at $28,350 MSRP and offer available bidirectional AC power. That means it does not need a separate wall-mounted inverter to supply over a projected 10 kW of AC power to a home.”

I’ll reach out to Ford for further clarification on that. In the meantime, Hart notes that the company expects its next-generation “Ford Home Power Station” EV charger to cost about half as much as the first-generation edition, which helps support the economic case for deploying an EV battery as a matter of routine, to take advantage of time-of-day utility discounts.

For drivers who plan to use their EV batteries as backup generators, Ford has also dropped the cost of its “Ford Home Backup Power Panel.” The final figure is not available yet, but Hart expects it to land at least 60% less than Ford’s first-generation backup power system.

Interested? Ford is taking names for Fathom pre-order invitations here.

If you’re wondering why Ford waited until now to remind everyone that the Fathom comes with a new, cost-cutting bidirectional charging system, that’s a good question. After all, it’s election season, and the media spotlight is focusing like a thousand points of light on the candidates and their various hi-jinks.

Still, there’s always room in the automotive press for something new and different. After all, Tesla CEO Elon Musk successfully dangled a shiny object with his Tesla “flying car” stunt on October 15. Slate Auto weighed in with a soft opening for its new showroom in Chicago, General Motors pitched its new EV platform alongside its new LMR battery formula on October 21, and the German automaker Audi topped them all over the weekend, when CEO Gernot Döllner appeared on stage with US President Donald Trump at a campaign rally in Georgia on Saturday.

For the record, during Saturday’s campaign stop Döllner and Trump announced that Audi will build a $4 billion factory in Georgia as its very first manufacturing venture in the US. The automaker has not released details, but over the summer Döllner indicated that the company’s US strategy includes plug-in hybrid EVs.

Whether or not those EVs are manufactured in Georgia remains to be seen. Döllner’s appearance with Trump on the eve of critical US elections provoked a firestorm of criticism in Germany, where critics pointed out that the supervisory board of parent company Volkswagen has yet to approve the plan.

Be that as it may, on October 24 Audi posted a press release describing the Georgia facility as “among the brand’s largest production footprints globally.”

Apparently that’s just for starters. “Audi looks forward to sharing additional U.S. investment announcements in the future as the brand continues to deepen its commitment to the American market,” the company added.

In the meantime, Audi is in damage control mode over the political optics of Döllner’s appearance with Trump. On October 26 the company posted an internal letter, presumably from Döllner, in which he attempted to explain why the automaker is creating new jobs in the US while laying plans to slash its workforce in Germany.

“The U.S. is the world’s second-largest premium automotive market. At the same time, our business there is currently not profitable. Many of our competitors have been producing locally for years. Our own local production will allow us to strengthen our competitiveness, reduce trade and currency risks, and better realize the market’s growth potential,” Döllner wrote.

“A stronger U.S. business is therefore not a decision against Germany. Rather, it is an important step towards strengthening Audi’s global competitiveness and helping to secure the company’s long-term success,” he continued.

Döllner also somewhat weakly acknowledged that the campaign appearance with Trump could be interpreted as a “political signal.” Nevertheless, Döllner did not back down from his stance.

“We remain firmly committed to Audi’s values and principles. Equally, we have a responsibility to safeguard the company’s economic future. We believe these responsibilities go hand in hand and together guide our actions,” he concluded.

Whether or not that explanation satisfies the Volkswagen supervisory board remains to be seen. Hold on to your hats…

#3538. Television Academy Foundation Pacts With Best Buy to Launch Production Assistant Training Program for Los Angeles Area High School Students

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Television Academy Foundation is launching a 10-week production assistant training program for Los Angeles-area high school students in a partnership with consumer electronics retailer Best Buy.

Through funding from Best Buy, the after-school program will be free for students, providing them with training for entry-level positions from PAs, production coordinators, assistant directors, production managers, producers, location managers and production accountants. Students will also take field trips to experience scripted and unscripted television productions firsthand. Related Stories 'Midday Meal' Review: Touching Iranian Drama Charts a Teacher's Desperate Acts to Provide for His School 'Ibuku' Review: Dreamlike Indonesian Drama Is a Compelling Tale of Parental Redemption

“We are thrilled to partner with Best Buy to offer this exceptional program to local underserved communities,” said Tina Perry, chair of the Television Academy Foundation. “This curriculum combines industry education, practical production training, professional mentorship and real-world exposure to help students build the knowledge, confidence and connections needed to begin exploring careers in television and entertainment.”

Television Academy board governor Donny Hugo Herran will serve as the program’s first instructor. The program will operate out of YMCA facilities in L.A.’s Boyle Heights neighborhood and in Long Beach. With 20 years of experience as an industry executive, Herran has steered production on a wide range of TV series, including scripted dramas “Yellowstone,” “Landman” and “MobLand” and unscripted shows such as “The Challenge” and “Martha & Snoop’s Dinner Party.”

Herran is also the author of “The Production Guru: Leadership Principles and Philosophies Across Film, Television and Beyond” and founder of Ellevan Creative Consulting.

“Opening doors to production jobs helps shape a more dynamic and representative entertainment industry,” Herran said. “I’m proud to lead the inaugural Production Assistant Training program, supported by Best Buy’s generous grant and to prepare aspiring crew members to help them thrive behind the scenes.”

#3624. Will ‘You Can See Everything’ Catch ‘Fahrenheit 9/11’ to Be the Highest-Grossing Documentary Ever?

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

It’s too early to say if Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim’s “You Can See Everything” will win Oscars or have a direct impact on history as it pertains to Elizabeth Holmes and Theranos, but it does feel like a movie that journalists are going to be referencing for a long time as a significant benchmark for documentary films at the box office.

Currently sitting at $45.1 million domestic, with no international release to speak of yet, A24’s film is already one of the highest-grossing documentaries ever. Domestically (not factoring inflation) it has already placed firmly within the top 10 and has obliterated films like “Free Solo” ($17.5 million), “Won’t You Be My Neighbor” ($22.8 million), and “An Inconvenient Truth” ($24.1 million). It has surpassed some big nature docs like “Earth,” “Chimpanzee,” and lags only “March of the Penguins” ($77.4 million), and it has surpassed concert docs like “Katy Perry: Part of Me” ($25.3 million) and “One Direction: This Is Us” ($28.8 million) but lags the Michael Jackson doc “This Is It” ($72 million) and “Justin Bieber: Never Say Never” ($73 million). Related Stories ‘The Mosquito Bowl’ Review: ‘Friday Night Lights’ Meets ‘Five Came Back’ in Peter Berg’s Formulaic Netflix Movie About a World War II Football Game ‘I Play Rocky’ Wins Virginia Film Fest Audience Award as Peter Farrelly’s Stallone Drama Readies for Theaters

If we want to put a bunch of really dumb caveats and qualifiers on it, “You Can See Everything” is already domestically the highest-grossing non-concert, non-political, non-nature docs ever made, a made-up distinction previously held, by my count, by “Won’t You Be My Neighbor?” (sorry Mister Rogers).

But even if it passes all those other movies, it might still fall short of the documentary that remains far and away the biggest box office hit for a doc ever, Michael Moore‘s 2004 film “Fahrenheit 9/11,” Moore’s scathing takedown of President Bush and the Iraq War as released in the heat of a massive election year.

Moore’s film did not win the Oscar (though it did win the Palme D’Or at Cannes) because it was ineligible after airing on TV ahead of the election, and it did not prevent George W. Bush from being re-elected to a second term, and it probably isn’t even Moore’s best film. But it’s worth remembering just how seismic an event the film was upon its release, not unlike the stir that initial cryptic, meme-worthy teaser of Fielder and Holmes was.

Moore was already a firebrand with instant name recognition thanks to “Bowling for Columbine,” which was previously among the highest-grossing documentaries ever. But “Fahrenheit 9/11” carried with it even more publicity and attention, including because of the fight to get it to screens in the first place. Disney famously blocked Harvey and Bob Weinstein’s Miramax from releasing “Fahrenheit 9/11,” leading the Weinsteins to buy the film back and then partner with Lions Gate and IFC Films to get the movie into theaters. It even had to do so within an incredibly quick turnaround time of 24 days to get prints made and get the film delivered to theaters. Even then, Lions Gate co-president Tom Ortenberg was predicting that the film was going to disrupt distribution and set the record as the biggest documentary ever.

He was right. “Fahrenheit 9/11” opened on just 868 screens on June 25, but it was the No. 1 movie in America with $23.9 million, beating out other new releases like “White Chicks” and “The Notebook.” “You Can See Everything” opened to a similar number of theaters but made only $15.6 million in that first weekend. By July 4 weekend it had expanded to nearly double the theaters and made another $22 million, and by its fifth weekend was over $100 million. The film ultimately played in theaters for 15 weeks into October ahead of the election. Domestically, it made $119.1 million and even made an additional $103 million abroad to bring its worldwide total to $222.4 million. “This Is It” actually has the edge worldwide with $267.9 million, but the bulk of that came from overseas audiences.

The film sparked serious headlines about whether it would damage Bush in the election, and it was a big enough media sensation that “Fahrenheit 451” author Ray Bradbury lashed out at Moore as a “jerk.” Other filmmakers had released parody documentaries against Moore, and it earned sharp criticisms from political pundits disputing its accuracy. Moore’s follow-up movie “Sicko” received an even larger budget of $9 million from the Weinsteins, and it too grossed above “Bowling for Columbine” with $24.5 million domestic.

Clearly the conditions under which documentaries are released in theaters have changed drastically since “Fahrenheit 9/11.” Such a film would most likely today open on streaming, and in today’s hyper-polarized political climate, might struggle to be platformed by gun-shy distributors and streamers. And the market for all documentaries in theaters has fallen drastically in the post-Covid era. “Melania” was previously the highest-grossing doc of 2026 with $16.6 million domestic, but that one is an outlier for a number of reasons.

For “You Can See Everything” to catch “Fahrenheit 9/11,” it would have to put up “Obsession” levels of word of mouth, continually notching up at the box office this coming weekend as it continues to add screens, and the film already tripled its screen count in Weekend 2. But a long theatrical run and an awards push could realistically get it into the 70s and help it push for the Top 5 among docs.

You can see everything, yes, but even we didn’t necessarily see that one coming.

The ball is in the 33-year-old's court when it comes to his long-term future, with the Bhoys "willing" to hand out a new deal if he can continue to prove his worth.

Oxlade-Chamberlain will have to impress Martin O'Neill across the remainder of the season in order to prove his worth moving forward.

Neither Viljami Sinisalo or Sam Johnstone have been able to establish themselves as the number one so far, and that could see Toth come through the door.
