#4533. The 8 Best Slip-On Walking Shoes, According to Our Editors

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Looking to get out the door quicker? Don’t let laces slip you up.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

SLIDING INTO A pair of slip-on walking shoes might be the easiest thing you’ll do all day. This footwear was built for speed and convenience, making it an accessory that deserves valuable real estate by your door.

Slip-on walking shoes have a rocky history—older versions had a looser fit, raising the risk of instability and friction, especially with higher mileage. But newer designs use no-tie laces, sock liners, pull tabs, and collapsible heels to create a custom fit that rivals what you’ll get with traditional laces.

These shoes are an obvious choice if you have dexterity issues, but they're also just convenient for everyone looking for an easy pair of walking shoes. Slip-on walking shoes also offer more structure than other laceless shoe designs, which has earned them a fanbase with podiatrists. “They are typically a much better option than flip-flops or slides because they provide more stability and keep the foot properly secured,” says Melissa Lockwood, DPM, a podiatrist at Heartland Foot and Ankle Associates in Bloomington, Illinois.

Nicole Graf, DPM, founder of Lexington Podiatry and associate professor at the University of Kentucky, also calls slip-on walking shoes a “huge step up” from flip-flops and slides.

Slip-on walking shoes aren’t for everyone. The fit can still throw some people off, and some simply prefer the tighter feel that laces deliver. But if you want travel-friendly shoes you can toss on in a second, these are a solid choice. As people who value quality, supportive footwear, Men’s Health editors have put in plenty of miles in slip-on walking shoes. The below check all the boxes.

The On Cloud 6 is an excellent example of how good a slip-on shoe can be. You can get in and out of this sneaker with no issue thanks to its wide opening, but the shoe’s elastic “speed” laces and sockliner create a snug fit once you’re in. (Those elastic laces can be adjusted to find your perfect fit.)

The sneaker uses Zero-Gravity CloudTec, which is On’s signature lightweight foam with hollow pods below for a cushioned, yet stable, feel underfoot. (Just be aware that those pods are prone to trapping small pebbles and debris.) A built-in flexible plate, called a Speedboard, helps push you forward with each step. This shoe also comes in a waterproof option, if you’re into rainy strolls. With a wide range of color options and an unobnoxious design, you can pair these with just about anything.

It’s tough to find a quality shoe under $100 right now, let alone one from a big brand. But the Adidas Cloudfoam Cuxxion Sock Shoes have some colorways under $50, making them an ideal choice for budget shoppers who don’t want to mess with laces.

This shoe uses Adidas’ signature Cloudfoam technology at the midsole for a plush, cushioned feel. Its Adiwear outsole is durable and grippy enough for most needs, too. But the real standout in this shoe is its elastic sock upper. It molds to your foot to create a surprisingly snug feel, even for narrow feet. The elastic sock collar supports that secure feel, although it may be too tight for people with wider ankles. The engineered knit upper is highly breathable, lowering the odds you’ll end your walk with sweaty feet. We appreciate the oversized pull tabs in front and back, making the process of getting these on and off seamless.

Feet come in all sizes, making it tough to find a slip-on shoe that works for everyone—and putting adjustable features at a premium. The Nike Pegasus 42 EasyOn has a specialized EasyOn lacing and bungee system up top to help you get the level of tightness you want, before locking it into place. (The lock and bungee combination takes some practice to get right at first.)

The shoe has a collapsible heel that springs back into place once you’re in, making the process of getting these on seamless. It’s also crafted with the same ReactX foam and Air Zoom unit as the standard—and popular—Nike Pegasus 42 running shoe, delivering an energetic feel under your feet. Convenient pull tabs at the heel sit flush against the shoe when they’re not in use, adding to the shoe’s overall sleekness.

Read More: Best Walking Shoes for Men Over 50

Stability shoes are designed to guide your foot with each step and prevent too much inward rolling as you go. But choosing a stability shoe usually means sacrificing a high level of cushioning, since a shoe that feels too plush may not be overly supportive. Hoka’s Skyward Laceless manages to create a stable, locked-in feel while still delivering plenty of the brand’s signature cushioning underfoot.

The Skyward Laceless has a stretchy upper that gently hugs your foot, creating a sock-like feel. The ankle and instep are equally snug, which can be a good or bad thing, depending on your foot size and width. This shoe has plenty of foam below for a slightly bouncy feel, but it still corrects your stride as you go. The integrated tongue pull tab is a nice touch, making for easier entry.

The Under Armour SlipSpeed Mega is packed with features you won’t find in most slip-on walking shoes, creating a versatile, unique sneaker. For starters, this shoe converts from a sneaker design to a recovery slipper with the help of a collapsible heel. (Under Armour refers to this as going from “speed mode” to “slip mode.”)

The shoe skips elastic laces or a sockliner upper in favor of Under Armour’s specialized BOA fit system, a 12-point dial that you can turn to adjust the width across the midfoot. The wide base creates a stable feel, while lots of foam cushioning underfoot delivers a plushness you’ll appreciate. A heads-up: This shoe is slightly heavier than others at 11.7 ounces. That’s a difference you may start to feel on long walks.

After a grueling workout, it feels borderline pampering to slide into the right recovery shoe. The Kane Footwear Active Recovery Shoe is armed with features that should make it a fixture in your gym bag.

This slip-on has strong arch support and a slight rocker shape to help ease pressure on the bottom of your feet as you walk. Internal nodes in the footbed help to promote blood flow and circulation down below, while the shoe’s surprisingly secure design helps prevent slipping. The Active Recovery Shoe is water-resistant with an airy design that allows you to take this in and out of the gym shower without ending up with sopping feet. Heel tabs in back make it simple to get in and out.

Merrell is known for its rugged, outdoor footwear and hiking shoes. While the Merrell Jungle Moc isn’t exactly a hiking shoe, it’s a solid option when you want good traction, whether you’re navigating rocky ground or a slippery surface. The Jungle Moc has been a bestseller for years, with its simple slip-on design and high level of cushioning.

Dual side stretch panels and an exaggerated heel tab make it simple to get in and out of these shoes. The suede upper is breathable, but water-resistant to keep you dry in nearly all conditions. The Jungle Moc has a cushioned EVA midsole for stability and comfort, too. While these shoes are easy to get on, the curved heel makes it harder to slide out—just something to be aware of.

Clogs admittedly aren’t the right style for everyone—some offer less support than sneakers and may slip around more than a standard shoe. But the sleek Crocs Dylan Clog is a solid option for people who want a clog style without some of the common issues that come with it.

The Dylan Clog has a deep heel cup to help lock your foot in, lowering the risk it’ll move around as you step. It’s also made from one piece of Croslite (Crocs’ signature material) to prevent wear and tear over time. This shoe looks like leather thanks to its subtle pebbled texture, but it’s water-resistant and dries quickly when wet. The only major downside is that it’s not very breathable in front. If you’re prone to sweaty toes, this may not be the shoe for you.

Not all shoes are created equal, but the best slip-on walking shoes have all of the supportive features you’ll find in standard lace-up walking shoes. “You mostly get what you pay for in these,” says Richard H. Graves, DPM, a podiatrist at Sol Foot & Ankle Centers in Long Beach, California. He suggests taking a pass at dirt-cheap slip-on walking shoes. “The cheaper ones are not going to provide enough support in the long run, even if they might feel good at first,” Graves says. “There should be enough structure to support your arch and the midsole should be firm and not too flimsy.”

Many high-quality slip-on walking shoes provide similar comfort to regular sneakers. Look for elements like a breathable upper, pull tabs to make them easy to get on and off, and a roomy toe box. Structured heel panels and extra padding in this area help to keep your foot secure, even without standard laces.

Traction for slip-on walking shoes will vary depending on the type of shoe you choose. Most will offer decent traction—meaning, enough so you don’t feel like you’re slipping on wet or damp pavement. But a more rugged shoe like the Merrell Jungle Moc has a grippier sole to help provide extra traction over rockier ground.

Yes, slip-on walking shoes can provide arch support. If this is an important quality for you, look for features like contoured footbeds and a stable midsole. You’ll also want a shoe that fits well and stays put once it’s on to ensure your arch support is consistent. “Make sure that you're not getting them too loose just to make it easier to slide on and off—then you'll be sacrificing support,” Graves says.

To find the best men’s slip-on walking shoes, MH editors tested the most recent and popular pairs on the market, evaluating each for support, comfort, and traction. Because these shoes are designed for walking, not just comfort, durability was also a key factor. To round things out, we consulted three podiatrists for their input on how to evaluate a slip-on walking shoe, along with the value of this style. Based on their advice and our experience, we chose these slip-on walking shoes.

#3471. Why Herc Holdings Rocketed Over 20% Today

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Shares of construction equipment rental company Herc Holdings (HRI +22.76%) rocketed 23.4% today as of 2:21 p.m. EDT.

Herc rallied on an exceptionally strong earnings report published this morning, with a beat on both the top and bottom lines, and an increase to full-year guidance.

Herc had been trading at a fairly low valuation relative to equipment rental peers, amid concerns about its high debt stemming from last year's acquisition of H&E Equipment Services. However, those fears were allayed by the company's resilient growth and fleet utilization last quarter.

In the third quarter, Herc saw total revenue rise by 4.6% to $1.36 billion; however, the company's bottom line showed much stronger growth, with adjusted (non-GAAP) earnings per share up a whopping 76.1% to $3.91. Both figures handily beat expectations.

The massive increase in profitability was due to a 12% increase in equipment rental revenue and a 3.6 percentage-point increase in overall utilization. The improved efficiency was due to the company's successful integration of H&E, which closed in June of 2025.

While local residential and non-residential construction has been sluggish, Herc has apparently landed enough "mega-projects" in areas like data centers and oil and gas to continue growing.

While higher interest rates can be a headwind for construction, higher borrowing costs may also lead more construction entities to lease equipment from companies like Herc rather than buy it outright. That may be playing a role here as well.

Today's solid and improving results led to a big move in the stock because Herc came into the day trading at a steep discount to other large equipment rental companies, such as United Rentals (URI +3.57%) and Sunbelt Rentals (SUNB +3.07%). This was due to concerns over Herc's debt load resulting from the H&E acquisition. However, today's results appeared to indicate the realization of both cost and revenue synergies, resulting in higher-than-expected profitability.

After today's leap, Herc stock trades at roughly 21.5 times 2026 adjusted earnings estimates and about 14.4 times 2027 earnings estimates. That's a very reasonable price to pay for the stock, as long as the company can continue to find projects to utilize its expanded fleet. Data center growth appears set to continue through next year, and if the housing slump ever recovers, that would be another tailwind for this well-executing industrial stock.

#4953. XPL Forms Falling Wedge; Big Unlock Shows Limited Sales

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Home » Altcoins » XPL Forms Falling Wedge; Big Unlock Shows Limited Sales

Plasma’s native token XPL is stabilizing around $0.072 as it builds a falling wedge pattern.

The Plasma (XPL) setup has drawn attention from traders watching for a potential reversal, even as the network continues digesting a major token unlock from late September.

Plasma (XPL) operates as a stablecoin-focused layer-1 built for payments and transfers. Its token is used for fees, staking, validator rewards, and network security.

After the September 25 unlock of roughly 1.67 billion XPL for the team members, research cited by Milk Road showed that about 70 percent of those tokens remained unmoved after 10 days.

Roughly 297 million moved to exchanges or trading desks while about 184 million went to other wallets. Circulating supply rose to around 4.5 billion tokens, or 45 percent of the total, with further monthly unlocks scheduled through September 2028.

The recent technical view by Crypto With Gopal highlights XPL forming a falling wedge while price consolidates near $0.072. Bulls need a clean break above the 0.080–0.085 zone to confirm a shift and open room toward higher resistance.

$XPL forming a falling wedge pattern 👀📉Price is stabilizing near $0.072 after an extended decline. Bulls need a clean breakout above $0.080–$0.085 to confirm a potential reversal and build momentum toward higher resistance 📈🔥🎯 Bullish target: $0.125⚠️ Losing $0.070… pic.twitter.com/6eTR1aPcPR

One outlined bullish target sits near $0.125. On the downside, a loss of the $0.070 support area could invite further pressure and extend the broader downtrend.

The limited movement of unlocked tokens after the September cliff suggests holders have not rushed to sell in size so far.

That relative restraint has not prevented the token from grinding lower through October, with recent daily ranges staying compressed and the price sitting well below earlier levels.

Monthly unlocks continue on a set schedule, adding a steady stream of new supply that the market must absorb alongside any broader crypto weakness.

For now, attention centers on whether XPL can hold the $0.070 area and push through the upper boundary of the wedge.

A sustained move above $0.080–$0.085 would strengthen the case for a bounce toward the $0.125 region, while inability to defend nearby support would keep the path of least resistance pointed lower.

The combination of restrained post-unlock selling and a tightening technical pattern leaves the token at a clear inflection point, with the next decisive move likely determined by whether buyers can reclaim the immediate resistance zone.

#3480. Amgen vs. Vertex Pharmaceuticals: Massive Pharmaceutical Scale vs. Steady Drug Revenue

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Amgen Inc. (AMGN +0.75%) primarily generates revenue by discovering, developing, and manufacturing therapeutic medicines for oncology and cardiovascular diseases.

It secured Food and Drug Administration (FDA) approval for an IMDELLTRA label update, and the Federal Trade Commission filed an amicus brief in a lawsuit concerning its Enbrel patents.

Vertex Pharmaceuticals (VRTX +0.68%) primarily generates revenue by discovering and marketing innovative treatments for patients with cystic fibrosis.

It finalized the acquisition of Crinetics Pharmaceuticals, while the FDA expanded the approved use of its Casgevy treatment to include patients as young as two years old.

Revenue here refers to the data provider's standardized income-statement revenue line item, which helps investors track the total sales a business generates before operating expenses are deducted.

The first thing to note when examining a company's revenue trend is seasonality: some times of the year have stronger buying patterns than others. That is evident in both companies' revenue bars in the chart, which may be surprising for pharmaceutical stocks. But the fourth quarter (in calendar years, which coincides with the fiscal years of each business here) tends to be the best for healthcare companies because consumers tend to load up on prescriptions and supplies after fulfilling their health insurance deductible before it resets in the first quarter. For most stocks, compare revenue year over year by quarter.

For fiscal 2026, Vertex Pharma sales should rise about 10% to $13.3 billion, with net income coming in roughly even with 2025.

The company continues to benefit from its focus on cystic fibrosis (CF). Its recently launched Alyftrek, a once-daily, next-in-class, triple-combination CF regimen, is seen encouragingly by investors. Alyftrek marks a notable improvement to the twice-daily dosage of Vertex's other CF products and was approved in Australia, New Zealand, Switzerland, the European Union, the U.K., and Canada over the course of 2025, after the U.S. approved it in 2024. Plenty more jurisdictions are evaluating the treatment today. In the U.S., label extensions (the expansion of FDA approval for use) mean Vertex can treat 95% of people with cystic fibrosis in the country today.

Vertex has expanded into treating other conditions. Povetacicept, an investigational recombinant fusion protein therapeutic, continues to show best-in-class potential as a treatment for three chronic autoimmune diseases: IgA nephropathy (IgAN), primary membranous nephropathy (pMN), and generalized myasthenia gravis (gMG). The FDA has granted its Breakthrough Therapy Designation for IgAN, with potential approval by Nov. 30 this year, as well as Fast Track Designation for pMN. If approved across the IgAN, pMN, and gMG indications, peak annual sales could reach $4 billion to $7 billion, according to equity analyst firm Argus.

As the chart shows, Amgen is not a fast-growing company by sales, but, given its scale, it should be able to achieve a roughly 3% revenue increase in 2026, to around $37.8 billion.

But sizable sales, even slow-growing ones, have their benefits. For one, its billions in net income could enable Amgen to pursue future growth by acquiring other drugmakers. The business also has six drug franchises that generate $1 billion-plus in annual revenue. There is real value there.

Late last year, Amgen became one of nine companies to reach a deal with the U.S. government to lower drug prices (Bristol Myers Squibb (BMY -0.10%), Merck & Co. (MRK +0.86%), and Gilead Sciences Inc. (GILD +0.25%) are also among the companies that signed a deal). Based on this agreement, Amgen will be exempt from Trump's pharmaceutical tariffs for three years, a positive, in exchange for the company reducing prices on its key migraine drug Aimovig and rheumatoid arthritis medicine Amjevita by about 60% and 80%, respectively, in a direct-to-consumer platform launched this month.

Data source: Financial Modeling Prep. Data as of Sept. 25, 2026.

#346. RAF Fairford plot ‘coordinated by handler working on behalf of Iran’

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Two men charged under National Security Act were directed by handler believed to have been working for Iranian intelligence, court told

A group of men were directed to drive vans loaded with petrol to Fairford RAF base by a handler known as Ali, who police believe was working on behalf of Iran, a court heard on Tuesday.

One of the five men tasked with driving to the base called police in the early hours of 27 September after the handler warned the group about a bomb in the vans, prosecutors told Westminster magistrates court.

Two men, Mohmed Abdulkarim, 24, and Nouman Essghir, 24, both appeared in court on Tuesday charged under the National Security Act 2023.

Abdulkarim, of London, was charged with assisting a foreign intelligence service. He is accused of buying the vans used in the Fairford incident on the instruction of Ali.

Essghir, also of London, was charged with entering a prohibited place for a purpose prejudicial to the UK, after initially being arrested at the scene near the Gloucestershire base on 27 September and then re-arrested on 20 October. He is accused of visiting RAF Fairford and filming around the facility on a video call with Ali before returning a month later in the convoy of vans.

Abdulkarim has been released on bail under conditions including the surrender of his passport, and Essghir has been remanded in custody. They will appear at the Old Bailey on 20 November.

Prosecutor Louise Gray alleged the defendants were in contact with an individual known as Ali who is now believed to be abroad.

She said that during the events of 27 September at the RAF Fairford base, five men who were arrested travelled in vans, parked and got out “and were told to do so by someone known as Mr Ali”.

One of them, she said, had claimed they were told there was a bomb in the van and called 999.

The court heard that the person calling stated that they were from London and that he was one of a group of five males. The caller told the police that they had been tasked to drive vans to the location for the purpose of a new business venture. When the vans arrived at the location, the organiser was in communication with them over the telephone.

Gray alleged he then told them to park the vans and to walk away from them. After they did that, the organiser told them to run as he had planted a bomb in the van. The caller indicated that he and his colleagues had run away from the vans and were now calling the police for help.

The defendants are believed to have known Ali for “a number of years”, she said.

Essghir is accused of travelling to RAF Fairford on 22 August and contacting Ali.

Abdulkarim is accused of buying mobile phones and collecting cash for Ali, as well as buying five vans including the three that were driven to RAF Fairford.

The court heard he had known Ali for 10 years and it was claimed the latter had been linked to a drone incident at the Israeli embassy.

Counter Terror Policing said they believed the foreign intelligence service involved in the case was Iranian.

The assistant commissioner Laurence Taylor, head of national counter-terrorism policing in the UK, said: “We have seen a range of incidents in recent months that we believe have been directed by the Iranian state, and as the director general of MI5 said just the other week, we are working together to intervene, identify and respond to that threat.

“The public can help us with this, and as always I would urge them to report anything that they’re worried about – those reports can and do make a difference.”

A 25-year-old UK-Iranian dual national who was also arrested on 20 October on suspicion of aiding Iran’s intelligence service has been released without charge but remains on police bail after being arrested on 1 October on suspicion of preparation of terrorist acts.

Five other men arrested during the investigation remain on police bail.

Frank Ferguson, the chief crown prosecutor for the CPS special crime and counter-terrorism division, said: “The Crown Prosecution Service has decided to prosecute two individuals with National Security Act offences following a Counter Terrorism Policing investigation into a high-profile incident involving RAF Fairford. We will continue to work closely with our partners as this complex investigation continues.”

#3489. RTX Has a $340 Billion Backlog and Trades at 32 Times Earnings. Is It the Best Defense Stock to Buy Right Now?

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

RTX's (RTX -1.46%) recent results strengthened the investment case, with management raising guidance for sales, earnings, and cash flow. It's probably the most attractive defense company on the market, with a $340 billion backlog and a long stream of commercial aerospace original equipment and aftermarket revenue, balanced with a relatively low-risk defense business. Still, is it the best-value defense stock?

I think RTX is better positioned than Lockheed Martin (LMT -1.92%), and commercial-aerospace-focused GE Aerospace (GE +3.20%) is a very strong company. I will use those companies as benchmarks for defense and commercial aerospace valuations to see whether RTX is a good value.

Unfortunately, RTX doesn't break out its defense and commercial aerospace earnings separately, but we know the Raytheon segment is almost entirely defense, and Collins Aerospace and Pratt & Whitney account for roughly 30% of revenue, so let's assume their operating profit is in line with revenue.

Let's put aside the current valuation of 32 times trailing earnings, and instead focus on what investors can expect in the future. Based on Wall Street analyst consensus from S&P Global Market Intelligence, for 2027 enterprise value (market cap plus net debt) over operating income, Lockheed Martin trades at 13.8 times operating income, and GE Aerospace at 25.1 times. Let's use these valuations as a proxy for RTX's defense businesses and its commercial aerospace businesses, so the former is valued at 13.8 times operating income and the latter at 25.1 times.

If 30% of RTX-Collins Aerospace and RTX-Pratt & Whitney is in defense and 70% in commercial aerospace then it follows that those segments should be valued at 70%*25.1+30%*13.8 = 21.7 times operating income.

Plugging these valuations into the table below, valuing Raytheon purely as a defense business, and utilizing the Wall Street consensus for each segment's operating income in 2027, gives the following:

Data source: S&P Global Market Intelligence.

Given that the market expects RTX to have net debt of $22.6 billion in 2027, the theoretical market cap ($274.27 billion-$22.6 billion) is $251.7 billion, or $186 per share, compared with the current price of $192.

These back-of-the-envelope calculations suggest RTX is fairly valued relative to its peers, but if you think it's a better business than Lockheed Martin, then it's a better stock to buy. However, it's more of a long-term buy-and-hold stock than one that screams "unbeatable value" right now.

#3056. Fantasy Football Film Room: We are witnessing the best version of Tee Higgins

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Fantasy football is inundated with stats and charts (guilty!), but the more context the film provides, the more reliable the data gets. That's why we have a 2.5-hour show called Stats vs. Film every Tuesday at 6 p.m. ET. That show is live — join us in the chat!

This (experimental) weekly column will look at some of the biggest storylines I picked up by grinding the film.

If there's a No. 1 reason why the Patrick Mahomes offense didn't reach the red zone for the first time in his entire career in Week 7, it was the awful pass-catcher play. There were four drops with room to run on each of them, and Mahomes had to work for the yardage they did hold on to.

Straight up, Xavier Worthy just isn't very good. His slight frame and unwillingness to battle for yards after the catch or throw contact at the catch point really limit his usefulness. That's not too surprising. What just boggles the mind is how infrequently Worthy looks like the fastest player on the field.

A slot go ball down the sideline was easily nuked by 220-pound S/LB Nick Emmanwori, and Worthy chopped up his feet trying to run an out route against CB Devon Witherspoon. These types of reps should be advantage Worthy. They aren't. He also had a drop on a crossing route that made Mahomes turn into a Surrender Cobra. If not for a couple of shallow sit routes and a screen, Worthy would've gone catchless against the Seahawks, even without Rashee Rice and Tyquan Thornton.

Worthy has been the WR41, WR27 and WR65 in half PPR with Rice sidelined recently. He's done that on WR48 usage and a 23% target share.

We'll probably look back at the first three weeks of the year when Travis Kelce was literally leading the NFL in yards after catch as the outlier of the season. Over the past month, Kelce is the TE18 on TE20 despite injuries to multiple WRs. On tape, Kelce looks just okay, but even more of his production now is slipping out after chip help or four seconds after the play as Mahomes runs around. That's fun. It's just going to come with far less consistency, especially when Rice is back. Kelce dropped a pass against the Seahawks, to make matters worse.

Mahomes was also without his top OTs. Josh Simmons is trending towards never playing another snap for the Chiefs, and his backup is holding on for dear life. There are too many whiffs, bull rush pressures, and an inability to pick up a stunter on tape from their fill-in left tackle. That problem likely goes unsolved, so the hits and extra time sprinting around for Mahomes will pile up. Their fill-in right tackle was equally as bad, but at least Jaylon Moore (concussion) will be returning sometime soon.

There's just not a lot to support Mahomes when the Chiefs are in pure dropback passes right now, and it'd be nice if the front office fully admits to it before the trade deadline passes. Jerry Jeudy as a man-coverage winner or Calvin Ridley as a veteran would at least be something.

Concepcion had a career-best game in Week 7 in terms of fantasy points (19.2 HPPR), expected points (15.2), targets (10), receptions (8) and yards (129). Most of it came after the catch while working on shallow and intermediate concepts over the middle. His initial burst and underrated pound-for-pound physicality make it happen.

As my good pal Josh Norris notes, Concepcion will add the extra couple of yards by fighting on contact. It's something he cherishes as a player according to his pressers, and it's felt on tape. Part of that fight is simply just running really fast, too. This is purely physics after all. Concepcion gets up to speed quickly, and defenders are constantly only able to get an arm on him rather than squaring him up or using their full body weight to bring him down. Almost every clip from above shows that.

Concepcion did drop an explosive Cover 2 hole shot and couldn't stack a fairly slow CB on a go ball, but those are volatile plays and Concepcion's underneath role is good enough to ride as an upside WR4. He's the WR41 on WR36 usage this month and is my favorite to lead the Browns in receptions and yards down the stretch. We'll see if he gets used in the red zone, however.

The good news is he ran a route on all four dropbacks outside of 11-personnel last week. He's not a traditional slot.

The Broncos are in three-RB committee hell right now, with rookie Jonah Coleman emerging from IR with a real role. J.K. Dobbins was the starter, played well on tape and converted the backfield's lone inside-the-five-yard-line opportunity, but he only had 4.8 expected half PPR points in a positive game script because Coleman stole the second half. Through three quarters, snaps were Coleman (15), Dobbins (13) and RJ Harvey (12). If Dobbins doesn't run well early, he'll lose the veteran nod in a battle for the hot hand. Without any late-down snaps last week and minimal receiving work to fall back on, Dobbins can't be started in fantasy, even if the Broncos are running the ball more with Sean Payton back on the sticks.

Here's a look at the Denver RB rotation from Week 7.

Coleman caught a couple of checkdowns while splitting late-down snaps with Harvey. His carries were up-and-down. He is not an above-average athlete or powerful rusher, and he did overthink some runs, in my opinion. That said, he did find some hidden yards, too. It was a passable but not a needle-moving performance. I'd be surprised if Coleman "Wally-Pipps" Dobbins off this outing. It'll likely take some time, barring injury. Coleman is a bench stash only, one that other fantasy football fans like far more than I do.

And finally, Harvey is still making plays in a very defined, niche role. He took a screen against a blitz to the house from 52 yards out, showcasing his best-in-the-backfield explosiveness. He also slipped out on a play-action target called for him, but that 19-yarder was called back. Harvey should keep splitting pass-down work with Coleman. That's not a fantasy football role, however. He had just 3.1 expected half PPR points in a blowout win against the dang Cardinals.

I fear that we're simply splitting 19.9 expected half PPR points into thirds, which means nobody can be trusted as an RB3/FLEX until we get a winner declared.

Here's a look at RB fantasy usage by team in 2026.

Higgins is on pace for 1,618 receiving yards. This month, he's the WR5 on WR16 usage. His tape is the best it's ever been, operating as a real deal No. 1 target on intermediate breaking routes on top of his signature catch-point moments near the sideline. He was outstanding against the Ravens' loaded secondary last week, finishing as the WR2 overall with 148 yards.

He had a 3rd-and-3 slant win that twisted up a press corner. He stacked Nate Wiggins on a go ball up the sideline. He stole a dig route by coming back to the ball against a zone LB. And he scored from the No. 3 spot in a trips set on a skinny post against Kyle Hamilton. Yahoo's Football 301 noted that Higgins had been positioned there just 14 times in his NFL career and the first time he was there this entire season:

Cincinnati leads the entire NFL with a 65% neutral pass rate (1st) and in success rate on throws of 10+ air yards. It's back to buttering its bread.

Here's a look at EPA per dropback versus success rate in 2026.

News broke on Tuesday that the Colts sent All-World OG Quenton Nelson to the Seahawks. Over the past three years, the Seahawks were 31st in pass block grade and 23rd in run block grade at their guard spots, per PFF. The Colts with Nelson were fourth and fifth, respectively. If just looking at this season, Indianapolis is first in total grade versus the Seahawks being 25th.

What's interesting about the trade is Nelson is a left guard, and that's where 2025 first-rounder Grey Zabel has played in the pros. Nelson isn't moving, so Zabel will either play center or right guard. He can play all spots and was considered a high-upside center prospect by many.

Anyway, what makes Nelson so good? This blog with OL expert Brandon Thorn shines a light on just that:

He called him the best LG in football, with a slight lean as being a better run blocker than pass protector. He'll fit into any scheme with ideal size, athleticism and length. He's one of the smartest and most disciplined linemen on top of it, so there should be few issues moving over midseason.

This will help Sam Darnold calm down in the pocket, but the pass game is so straightforward with Jaxon Smith-Njigba dominating targets again. This upgrade will not be enough for Rashid Shaheed, Cooper Kupp or AJ Barner to be fantasy relevant.

Where this will be felt most is in the run game. The Seahawks are currently dead last in yards before contact (0.3) on runs between the B gaps, and the next worst team is up at 0.8. That's a huge gap for Emanuel Wilson in the short term and Zach Charbonnet and Jadarian Price in the long term. I'd expect the Seahawks to keep using a rotation at RB, and the best runner will be the one who ends up fantasy relevant by the time the fantasy playoffs get here. I'd hold out some hope for Price over Charbonnet.

As for the Colts, Jonathan Taylor faces an even higher uphill battle. It's possible he's traded next, with Indianapolis also figuring to trade away star DT DeForest Buckner. The winner would be Seth McGowen, JT's backup, if Taylor is moved. The rookie will be a starter. Will the team be good? Of course not, but finding a starting RB on the waiver wire for free is always good business.

Nation’s Dumbest is the first television show where the loser is the winner. Celebrities fight it out to be eliminated from the competition as quickly as possible to avoid being crowned the nation’s dumbest. #3507. S&P 500 Sell-Off: Is Now the Right Time to Buy the Dip?

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The stock market looks a bit wobbly. After reaching a new record high on Oct. 6, the S&P 500 index (^GSPC +0.60%) has declined by about 2.5% since then (as of this writing).

On the one hand, a 2.5% drop in the S&P 500 can be unnerving to some investors. It might be a sign of economic trouble ahead. What if the stock market plunges even further into a 10% correction or a 20% bear market?

On the other hand, many investors might see a 2.5% price decline in the S&P 500 as a sign that "stocks are on sale" and that it's time to buy more shares of S&P 500 exchange-traded funds (ETFs). This is called "buying the dip" -- investing more money in the stock market when the overall market has "dipped" to lower levels.

Here's the thing: If you can patiently leave your stock portfolio alone for the next several years, asking if it's time to "buy the dip" is the wrong question. For consistent, long-term investors, there is rarely a bad time to invest.

Here's why patient, consistent, "boring" long-term investing is often more lucrative than waiting for the perfect moment to swoop in and "buy the dip."

The S&P 500 has delivered average annual returns of 9.98% since 1928. That's a remarkably long-term track record of wealth-building returns despite the massive stock market crashes, global wars, and short-term economic crises along the way. The stock market tends to keep chugging along. Companies make money and pass on some of those earnings to shareholders. Stocks go up.

The past few years for the S&P 500 have been even better than that long-term 98-year average. In the past 10 years, the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO +0.58%) has delivered annualized returns of 15.3%. In the 16 years since the fund's inception, it has delivered annualized returns of 14.94%.

If you just bought shares of VOO and held onto them for five, 10, or 16 years, you would've earned significant returns. Even if you thought you were investing at a "bad time" or the "best time" on any given day.

When the S&P 500 is down by a few percent in a month, it's easy to think that "maybe I'll just wait to invest until stocks go down even more. Then I'll buy more shares at a lower price." But this is called market timing, and it doesn't work.

Research from Schwab found that, based on 20 years of S&P 500 returns from 2005 to 2024, someone who invested $2,000 per year as early as possible each year during those 20 years would have earned almost as high a return as someone who invested $2,000 per year at exactly "the right time," on the day of each year when S&P 500 levels were lowest.

Even if you perfectly "buy the dip" on the S&P 500 (which is impossible to do consistently), you still might only slightly outperform the returns you'd get from steady investing over many years.

Don't worry about buying the dip or timing the market or picking the perfect short-term moment to invest when the S&P 500 is volatile. Just focus on your long-term plans. Buy and hold a diversified portfolio of great stocks, and let the market work for you.

Ben Gran has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Vanguard S&P 500 ETF. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

*Average returns of all recommendations since inception. Cost basis and return based on previous market day close.

#3587. Why Rocket Lab Stock Took Flight on Tuesday

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Rocket Lab (RKLB +8.34%) stock took flight on Tuesday, climbing as much as 10%. As of 2:51 p.m. ET, the stock was still up 8.4%.

The catalyst that sent the rocket specialist higher was the latest news about its Neutron rocket.

In a post on X today, Rocket Lab announced that the Neutron rocket's second stage -- which is responsible for delivering payloads once in orbit -- and the accompanying thrust module had departed the factory for the Virginia launch site.

It’s a beautiful day to set sail with the next pieces of Neutron flight hardware to Launch Complex 3. Earlier today we sent Neutron’s Stage 2 and Thrust Module out of the Neutron Factory and onto a barge for transport through Chesapeake Bay to Wallops Island in Virginia. Once it arrives, the Thrust Module will go straight onto the launch mount at LC-3 for check outs and the beginning of wider systems demonstrations including integrated fluids tests and module-to-launch-mount interface checkouts. With no strongback on the launch mount by design, Neutron’s Thrust Module is the rocket’s only physical interface to the launch pad, which this upcoming test campaign enables us to test early before the entire vehicle comes together.

In the wake of this announcement, Cantor Fitzgerald analyst Andres Sheppard reiterated his overweight (buy) rating and $122 price target. That suggests potential gains for investors of 127% compared to Monday's closing price. The analyst expects a Neutron launch in the second quarter of 2027, giving investors the opportunity to profit from the recent sell-off.

Earlier this month, Rocket Lab announced that it would delay Neutron's inaugural launch. The flight, originally scheduled for year-end, was delayed until 2027 after the company discovered a defect in the propellant dome.

Rocket Lab stock has been beset by volatility, exacerbated by the recent IPO of Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +1.14%) -- aka SpaceX. Ahead of the company's public debut, Rocket Lab shares took flight, gaining 115% during the first five months of 2026, but have since plunged more than 61% (as of this writing).

Given the boom-or-bust nature of spaceflight, volatility is here to stay, especially since we're still in the early stages of the "space race." At 44 times sales, Rocket Lab stock isn't cheap, but it is less expensive than SpaceX, which sells for 61 times sales. Moreover, as the space race matures, Rocket Lab is poised to shoot for the stars.

Danny Vena, CPA has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Rocket Lab. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3202. Margin debt growth slows sharply as stock market warning signs build

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The pace at which investors are borrowing money to invest in stocks has cooled substantially, raising concern that the stock market may be due for a fall.

Recent FINRA data shows the annual growth in margin debt fell to 31.4% in September from 53.7% in May, Bank of America technical strategist Paul Ciana pointed out in a Friday note. Ciana warned that slowing borrowing momentum from extreme levels has been associated with previous market peaks.

May's growth rate in 2026 was the fourth highest since 1998, according to a BofA analysis. The three higher extremes took place in 2000, 2007 and 2021 — all of which preceded stock market declines.

Earlier this year, borrowing was increasing much faster than stocks were rising. Analysts view high borrowing levels as a contrarian signal, similar to bull-bear sentiment indicators.

At $1.48 trillion, margin debt in September is 1.5% below June's record of $1.5 trillion.

High leverage can have snowball effects in a market downturn. Lower prices trigger demands for additional collateral or forced sales, which accelerate a downturn.

Individual investors can be hit hard. Tesla investor Hy Luu, for example, refinanced his home to raise $30,000 to pay down margin debt during the stock's 2022 decline.

BofA sees other market warning signs, including widening high-yield credit spreads and a rotation toward defensive sectors like healthcare and consumer staples.

Paradoxically, the rotation into those sectors has pushed them to "bubble-risk levels," BofA's equity derivatives team said in a note on Tuesday. Similar shifts occurred in the late 1990s dot-com era and Covid rally, they added. "These dynamics provide further evidence that markets continue to move towards a more bubble-like state."

The bull case is that the midterms could alleviate some of the market's uncertainty and trigger a relief rally, Ciana noted. "Typically, risk assets such as the S&P 500 rebound after midterms unless more severe downside risks materialize."

#4324. GM Didn't Want Hybrids. Now It's Putting Them In Its Biggest SUVs: Report

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Browse this and many other websites without banner ads, personalized tracking and video ads.

General Motors once envisioned leapfrogging hybrid technology and jumping straight into an all-electric future. Not too long ago, it announced plans to join the hybrid boom in the U.S.—and now we know where the powertrain may land in its lineup.

GM is planning to build hybrid versions of the Chevrolet Traverse, ​Buick Enclave, and GMC Acadia three-row SUVs, Reuters reported on Tuesday, citing people familiar with the plans. GM itself announced on Tuesday that it will build hybrids at a Lansing, Michigan, factory, adding that "details about nameplates and timing will be shared closer to launch." All three models are built in Lansing.

The automaker ran hard at electric vehicles over the last several years, developing one of the country's broadest battery-powered lineups. But it sells just one low-volume hybrid in the U.S., the Chevrolet Corvette E-Ray. It's behind Ford in that regard, which has a relatively narrow hybrid lineup itself but sells trucks like the Maverick Hybrid and F-150 Hybrid in large numbers. Stellantis killed off its lineup of plug-in hybrids after fuel-economy regulations fell away and it too has lagged behind on regular hybrids as companies like Honda, Hyundai, and especially Toyota sell increasing numbers of them.

In early 2024, GM first signaled a pivot away from its all-in-on-EVs strategy. CEO Mary Barra mentioned that the company would bring back plug-in hybrids on an earnings call. Since then, GM has reaffirmed plans to sell hybrids in the U.S.—"we're not tone-deaf to our consumers," one executive told CNBC not too long ago—without saying exactly which vehicles would get the treatment.

Hybrids came about around the turn of the 20th century and stagnated at about 2% of the U.S. car market for years. But recently they've been booming as EVs have faltered. In the third quarter, hybrids made up 16% of car sales in the country, beating the roughly 6% share claimed by EVs.

#5900. One of the Most Unusual Jobs at Sea? Looking After 24 Cats and Dogs

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

This is the latest installment of our bimonthly column Crews on Cruise, spotlighting the people who work behind the scenes of the world’s most memorable voyages—from bartenders and entertainers to ship captains and expedition leaders.

Oliver Cruz has one of the more unusual jobs at sea: He is the kennel master aboard Cunard’s Queen Mary 2, a 2,759-guest ocean liner that has been making transatlantic crossings between Southampton and New York since 2004. It is also the only cruise ship in the world to offer dedicated kennels at sea during its transatlantic voyages, with 24 spaces for dogs and cats traveling with their owners.

Cruz joined the ship in 2011, initially as a spa attendant. But when the housekeeping department learned that he had spent eight years back home in the Philippines working as a zoo guide and animal handler, with everything from snakes to crocodiles, he was moved to the kennels. He has been there ever since. “Dogs are angels,” Cruz says. “Not all angels have wings. Some of them have fur.” No surprise then that Cruz has 15 dogs of his own at home and is a member of the Philippine Animal Welfare Society.

His charges aboard Queen Mary 2 have ranged from teacup Yorkies to a Bernese mountain dog whose three daily meals each included roughly three pounds of minced meat and more than 10 ingredients. Some are regulars: Cruz has cared for one dog on 32 crossings. By now, he can rattle off their names, personalities, dietary quirks, and preferred sleeping arrangements, and even remembers which “house,” as he calls the kennels, his repeat guests slept in on previous voyages.

One occupational hazard: Cruz estimates he has more than 50 dog bites on his body, nearly all sustained while diving in to break up fights. “Nobody bites me intentionally,” he says. Experience has taught him that “the way you treat a husky is not the same way you treat a Shih Tzu.”

We caught up with Cruz earlier this year to talk about life in the kennels, his unusually demanding clientele, and why managing their human parents can sometimes be the hardest part of the job. As Cruz puts it, “You need the knowledge, but you also need the heart."

What does a typical day on the job look like?“On embarkation day, we go down to the terminal and first cross-check all the paperwork. There are very complicated requirements, especially coming into the UK, so we check things like the stamps and the timing of vaccinations.

From there, we bring the dogs up to the kennels and explain the visiting hours and the dos and don’ts to their owners. The dogs have an outdoor area we literally call the poop deck, with an American fire hydrant for the American dogs and a British lamppost for the British dogs. For the sake of their holiday, I let them experience both.

Owners can leave toys, blankets, even used pajamas or socks inside the houses so the dogs can smell them and feel more comfortable. If two dogs live together and get along, we take out the partition so they can cuddle overnight.

Then there's the feeding. Every dog has its own portion sizes and diet, and it all has to be right. The food ranges from boiled chicken breast to grilled steak, steamed salmon, Cumberland sausage, scrambled egg, fruits, vegetables—basically whatever they need. The food comes from the same kitchen that cooks for the guests, but I am very strict: no salt and pepper.

Sometimes people who don’t have pets in the kennels try to come in because the dogs are cute—maybe 20 or 25 people in a day. I have to explain that the dogs are already trying to adjust to an unfamiliar environment as well as the other dogs. They don’t need more strangers coming in to see them.”

What is your favorite part about this job?“Meeting all the new dogs on the first day and making my family bigger. After seven days of bonding, playing, and running around together, saying goodbye is the saddest part.”

What is the most challenging part about working at sea?“The parents. Sometimes I tell the owners, ‘No treats,’ but I can see them secretly feeding their dog oily food like bacon from the kitchen. I have to say, ‘Mama, I’m not so happy seeing you do this.’

And once I had somebody take their own dog back to their cabin without telling me. That evening, I was about to close the kennel when I did my usual count and realized one was missing. It was a very stormy evening, and I was running around like a headless chicken. I was soaking wet and crying. I asked housekeeping to send people around the ship to help me find this dog, but nobody could.

Finally, I phoned the owner and she said, ‘Oh yes, Oliver, she’s here.’ I was dying that night!”

What is the coolest or weirdest thing about living on a ship?“The weirdest thing: When I’m home, I feel strange. When I’m here, I feel at home.

I play drums and guitar in the Filipino crew band, and we perform in the crew bar. We play a lot of Red Hot Chili Peppers. I even got to play with Travis Barker here on Queen Mary 2. They were filming something on the ship and shot us playing together. I remember singing ‘All the Small Things’ and ‘What’s My Age Again?’ That was one of the coolest things.”

What is your favorite place you’ve traveled so far?“Liverpool. I’m a big Beatles fan. I have John Lennon tattoos all over my body. When I’m there, I visit Mendips, John Lennon’s childhood home, Strawberry Field, and Quarry Bank School. And I always spend the night at the Cavern Club, sometimes until two in the morning. That’s my place.”

What is your favorite place to hang out on board the ship?“The kennels, honestly. It’s a different experience staying here for seven days, getting to know each other’s dogs.”

Tell us about a memorable guest interaction that sticks out to you.“One of the most memorable was a dog named Paisley. She had come from a shelter and, according to her owner, had been mistreated before. She was aggressive and would bite, and she was having a difficult time in the kennel.

When the other dogs were put away, I would let Paisley out and spend time with her alone. I told her, ‘Paisley, you are not the boss. I am the boss.’

One day, her owner came in and saw Paisley sitting on my lap and licking my face. She started crying and said, ‘What did you do to my dog?’ That was the start of a very good relationship. I will never forget her.”

What’s your top tip for first-time cruisers?“Always remember that you and your dog are reflections of each other. So you need to be calm and happy. If you are really nervous, the dog will feel that nervousness too.

And for cats, give them time. Cats are the boss. They don’t trust places and people easily, so do not force them. One day, the cat will come to you. Once they become talkative and start asking me for attention, I know: That’s it. We are friends.”

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.
